The Easter volunteer mancevres which have been conducted round London during the holidays have be n carried on by three brigades at places as far apart as Woking, C.llchester, ancl Canterbury. They have been more or leas interfered with by the weather, but a good deal .of valuable work has nevertheless been doce. The general idea for the day's operations at all'the three Places was that three forces were moving from different points to- wards the metropolis,and the practice varied accord- ing to the character of the country and the other conditions of wind and weather. The brigade operat- ing at Woking had the honour of being inspected by the Duke of Connaught and General Trotter. Lord Jalmouth, who commanded atColchester, has expressed the greatest satisfaction with the intelli- gent manner in which the work was carried out, and in Kent, in the Canterbary quarter, Lord Bel- haven seems to have been equally well pleased. Both at Chatham and Sheerness the artillery and engineers had some good practice, the latter being drilled in boarding cutters, pinnaces, rafts, and horse-boats, and in crossing the river.

The decline in the number of members of the to- tal abstinence section of the Church of England Temperance Society has caused the Executive Com- mittee great anxiety,as the fall is very substantial. During the last year abaut 10,000 have disappeared from the roil; and.taking the decrease over a period of six or seven years, it amounts to upwards of 2300. '1 he Executive Committee, which met in the middle of last week,appointed a sub-committee to inquire into the cause of this phenomenon. It can scarcely be attributed to laxity in the registra- tion ef members, or to defective returns, for it may be assumed the., if these causes are operative they were equally operative six years ago. But, what- ever the cause, it is well to know the facts, and an inquiry is the first step to a remedy.

A correspondent who has just visited the co- operative colony at Clousden Hill, near Newcastle- on-Tyne, informs me that the number of resident workers has been reduced to seven, exclusive of two wougen and four children. This is•to be accounted for, not by any falling off in the supply of would-be colonists. but by a change of policy in the management of the settlement. There is no longer an open door for anyone prepared to sub- scribe to the "statement of principles.' It is per- haps too much to say that the principles of "free communion" upon which the colony was founded have been abandoned, but those members with whom my correspondent conferred did not hesitate to admit that the ideals of the promoters had been considerably modified by experience—in fact, at no distant date it is probable the farm will be run on a frankly commercial basis of co-operative produc- tion. Meanwhile the work is hard,but the workers are confident, not as to the ultimate triumph of free communist association, but as to the material success of their enterprise.

When Sir William Harcourt poked fan in the House of Commons at the math etic pretensions of the new police buildings erected by a distinguished architect in the neighbourhood of the Houses of Parliament, he was probably expressing the senti- ments of ninety-nine men in a hundred. The House of Commons itself certainly agreed with him. It is a significant circumstance, moreover, that the Liberal leader's opinion in matters of taste is evid- ently felt to be oi more weight than that of Lord Salisbury. for when the Prime Minister, a year or two ago, took occasion to indulge in a little light banter at the expense of this identical edifice, not a word of protest WdB raised. Now, on the other hand, Sir William Harcourt finds himself for his criticism formally excommunicated from the circle of the elect, and is sharply told to confine his attention in future to things he understands. Sir William will at least bays the sympathy of those honest Philis- tines who are Just as much puzzled by ths flatter- ing epithets lately showered on Mr. Shaw's build- ing as they are by the fiery condemnation in Rue- kin's pages of Barry's rococo masterpiece—the neighbouring Houses of Parliament.

A really serious piece of Vandalism has been sug- gested in the form of a scheme for widening Water- loo Bridge. The proposal is to add to the width by equipping the viaduct on each side with a new foot- path, supported by buttresses to spring from the existing piers. In this way the whole width of the structure as it now is would be converted into a roadway for vehicular traffic, and at the same time the appearance of one of the most perfectly pro- portioned bridges in Europe would be hopelessly ruined. The scheme, however, is not likely to be adopted. It is a curious fact that until recent years the construction of bridges in London, with the ex- ception of those in the city, was a matter of private enterprise. Most of the great bridges across the Thames were not acquired by the public authorities and freed from toll till 1879. Waterloo, which was erected between 1814 and 1817, cost a million ster- ling to build, while Vauxhall—the first iron viaduct by which the river was ever bridged—cost at about the same period a little over a quarter of that sum. This old iron structure, which has neither beauty net convenience to commend it, is now about to be demolished, and in course of time its place will be taken by a successor In the style of the bridge at Westminster.

Mr. John Bi rns has found a fitting theme for his rolling periods, when the House meets, in the griev- ance of the poor theatrical "supers," who are being ousted from employment by the use ef soldiers at some of the theatres. This will lead to some eharp questioning of Mr. Brodrick, and some pungent criicism when the Army Estimates are again in Supply. The grievance opens up the whole point which has been so frequently urged by Mr. Burns and several other membsrs—unfortunately not Labour ones—in the case of ths employment of retired soldiers enjoying pensions, in the work of the Post-office and other State, and even in private, establishments. The case of the "supers" is even worse than the case of the pensioners, for the so!- Wen are in active State employment and on full State pay, such as it is, and their engagement as theatrical supers is not a legitimate competition with civil labour. As regards pensioners or reserv- ists, the difficulties of the State must be acknow- ledged, as the men are once more thrown back upon civil life with an inadequate means of support, but no such argument applies to soldiers serving with the colours.

Decisive measures have at length been taken by the Wesleyan authorities in London in regard to the valuable property in Bishopsgate-street, concerning which so many rumours have been afloat of late. These have arisen largely as the result of the secrecy which has been observed whilst the future of the Centenary hall and Mission-house was under con- sideration. There is truth in the statement that an enormous sum Was offered for what is probably one of the finest sites in the City of London. The authorities have, however,flnally come to a decision to retain it for Methodism in perpetuity. The huge unsightly pile which fronta the point at which Threanueedle-atreet enters Bishopgate-street is, however, to be entirely taken down, and an isipos. lag structure suited to the modern requirements of Methodism is to sake its place. The outlay will be costly, reaching at least £70.000 ; but City experts encourage the hope that, apart from all other ad- vantages, it will financially be a great success. It is not intended that this important reconstruction scheme shall interfere with Mr. Perks's Wesleyan Methodist Church-house, for the erection of which a quarter of a million is proposed to be taken from the million-guinea scheme. Architects have been instructed to draft the necessary plans, after which the project will be pushed forward with the eas possible delay.

The executive of the co-operative movement have been taking the opinion of the various branch or- ganisations as to whether they are desirous of separate representation in Parliament, and whether they are willing to contribute t a Parliamentary maintenance fund. It is already known, though the actual result has not been given t) the public, that the answer will be in the affirmative. The frundly attitude of the Trades Congress Parliamen- tary Commiaee towards the proposal has been cured .

A FARMER was noted among his friends for grumbling ways. One year the crops were very good, and some curiosity was felt to see how he would meet the case. 'I am afraid," said he, "that such a great crop will be a powerful strain on the land."

JOHNNY came in with one eye in mourning, a handful of hair gone, his face smeared with dirt hand tears, and his clothes soiled and torn beyond description. "I've been a-fightion' 1" DuaiNG the time Swift held the living of Laracor, he was out riding one day, when he met one of his parishioners, a farmer, capitally mounted. "That's a first-class animal you ye got there." "Mr. Dean," replied the farmer, "he is very well, but he is not equal to yours." "To mine!" returned Swift. "Why, he's a mere pad." "Aye," replied the other, "but he carried the best head of any horse in Ireland."

Suna is Penn.—The modern history class was in session. "Willie," asked the teacher, "who was Gladstone?" "Excuse me, Miss Bulger," said Willie, "but oughtn't that to be who is Gladstone!" "Possibly," replied the teacher, somewhat abashed. "But—who is Gladstone?" "He's the man"— began Willie. "Yes," said Miss Bulger, encouragingly. "He's the man after whom the Gladstone bag was named," said Willie. ONE of the stories of the late Sir Benjamin Ward Richardson's was of a visit to one of the few small towns in England which have no 'public-houses. Although there were four thousand people there, the doctor was nearly starving. One day a young medical man came to Sir Benjamin for advice as to taking the practice, and Sir Benjamin placing his hands on the young doctor's shoulder, said: "Take my advice, and don't. Those wretched teetotallers heal so fast that there is no profit in them." HE WaNxnn To KNOW. Little Clarence: "Pa." Mr. Callipers: "There, my son; stop right where you are. A CERTAIN CusE.—A farmer who had recently lost some cows by the catt!e plague was fully per- suaded that he himself had been attacked by the epidemic. He consulted an eminent physician, who tried to laugh him out of the notion. The farmer, not being satisfied, went to another physician, who examined him carefully, and told him he could cure him. He wrote a prescription, sealed it up, and told the farmer to go to a certain druggist in the town. The farmer lost no time in going with it, but was somewhat startled when the druggist showed it him. He had good cause; it ran thus: " This man has the cattle plague. Take him into the back yard and shoot him, according to the Act of Parliament."

REMARKABLE STATEMENTS.

The" Melbourne Argus "publishes the following con- densed report of an interview which Prince Ranjiteinhji had with the representative of a Sydney newspaper after the last test match of the tour :—Ranjitsinhji has con- firmed the report of an interview with him published in the "Evening News." In it he speaks of the treatment to which the members of the team have been subjected. He bears out Stoddart's statements that the behaviour of the spectators has been most trying,and of such a na- ture that even a passive man like Stoddart was at last compelled to speak. In every place where the team went they had been insulted. He did not say it had been done maliciously, but rather for the sake of making a sensation. That was the most charitable construction to put upon it. But while, of course, a little chaff and ' banter was expected, it became a bit monotonoidlto be' hissed and hooted on every ground. When aided if members of the team felt so deeply this treatment that some of them had decided not to again visit Australia Ranjitsinhji said that some of the members certainly had an opinion of that kind,and among the professionals who had to make their living out of the game, the feeling was very strong. It was difficult to get an idea outside of what those on the field had to stand. In the match just played MacLaren and Bruce, when on the outfield, were pelted with insults, and finally Druo3 told Stoddart that the way in which the crowd talked at him the whole time made it unbearable to be in the outfield. It was no consolation to them that Australians were treated in the same way. What he felt was that it wane pity such a grand game should be spoilt by such conduct. It was a great consolation, however, to them to know that, despite the way in which their feelinge had been wounded by a section of the spectators, the utmost goodwill existed between the Australian players and themselves. The behaviour of the crowd at Sydney was at its worst on the last two days of the match. They , were especially bad after the leg-before appeal against , Darling. Storer then wont to him and said. " How I can I keep wickets with this mob howling." Hayward also moire of it, and there was not a member of the team who was not discomposed. To show what the visiting cricketers have bad to put up with, the Prince said that the public were constantly inventing and circulating all sorts of atrocious statements of dissension and disunion among members of the team. "At Adelaide," he con- tinued, " someone, not a gentleman, stated that I was putting on airs with the amateurs of our team, and that dissension was the result. He implied also that Mac- Laren and myself had had a fight, and that the team was in a general muddle. The whole of this was actually cabled to England, but fortunately it was stopped in time by a subsequent cablegram contradict- ing everything. Such a statement would have done our side moral harm there, and would have done me per- sonal harm as well. Then there was a rumour of a difference between MaeLaren and Stoddart. That was not enough, but MacLaren and Hirst must have a quarrel—a thing unheard of. Even this was not suffi- cient, but Druca and I must have a fight. When they grew sick ef saying we were prize-ightere. they started rumours to theeffect that we were taking an extra glass or two and not keeping ourselves in form. Of course such reports are absolutely untrue and unkind. There was no foundation for them whatever. Then the status of our amateur players has been called into question. Stoddart and I were receiving 13,000 each, and others large BUM as well. Why, if that were so,the promoters of the tour would find themselves thousands of pounds out of packet instead of having a balance. Would you believe it," the Prince went on, " that every member of our team who has happened to fail has received by next morning's post twenty or thirty telegrams and letters congratulating them on their poor scores. This has been the experience of every man in the team. Then we have also received other letters of congratulation of a different kind when we have been successful. But for all these insults, the charm of the tour is that we have seen Australian cricket, and you will probably see my Impressions of the tour in a future edition of my book." In conclusion, Ranjitsinhji said he had not once denied the superiority of the Australians. "In the first test match they were at their worst, while the English team was at its best. At the end of each of the other matches he had distinctly said that the Australians' showed better form, and he now repeated what ha bad then said and what he had published in the Review of Reviews ' about the better form displayed by the Australians."

THE EXAMINER HILDA Y APRIL 15, 1898

(Copyright.)

Neither spoke again for a few minutes. Dennis produced his cigarettes and selected one in a meditative manner. Then he brought out his match-box, which liappened, however, to be empty. Grace, whose eyes had followed all his movements, instantly rose. , „ "I will get you some matches, Dennis," she aid. "Oh, don't trouble, thanks. I can get 'them myself," he answered. • But she had already opened a cabinet drawer, and bringing out a box, handed it to him. "Thank you," he said, taking it. "Yon should have let me get them myself, though." "I like doing little things for you," she quietly observed. -

He made no reply, but turned slightly away, as if her thoughtfulness irritated him from some in- comprehensible cause. Again both lapsed into silence. Grace returned to her place on the sofa, and her husband remained near the window, smoking his cigarette in a pre- occupied manner. After a while he turned round and said, speaking as a man who faces an un- pleasant duty: - _

"Grace, lain sorry lam forced to reopen that conversation about Miss Treherne's prosecution. Her trial comes on the day after to-morrow, so you will see that I have put off mentioning the matter as long as I possibly )could." "But, Dennis, I thought it was all settled!" she gasped, turning white, "and that the prosecution was at an end."

"At an end l" he repeated with surprise. "Who told you that ? " "I—l—heard it," was the hesitatingly spoken reply. "Unfortunately there is no truth in the state- ment. Miss Treherne was committed for trial at the second hearing before the magistrates, a true bill "%vas returned against her by the Grand Jury, and—as I said just now—her trial takes place the day after to-morrow. Judging from the evidence which the prosecution is bringing forward, I'm afraid that there is little or no chance of her being acquitted. She has no witnesses." "But I thought it was all settled," repeated Grace, as if at a loss what else to say. "I only wish that you were right, and that my responsibilities in the matter were over. If she is convicted, she will be sentenced to imprison- ment—perhaps for a long period. Do you not fully understand the cruelty of allowing an innocent person to be sent to prhion ? " Grace shuddered as her husband uttered the word "prison," which he noticed. Rising, he threw away the end of his cigarette and took a chair nearer her.

"All circumstances considered," he began, "I cannot, as an honourable man, remain silent with regard to certain suspicions. I must let the defence know what—" he stopped, then added: "Grace, you must tell me this evening all you know about that forged cheque." "But why do they insist on saying Miss Treherne cashed it? she answered evasively. "Because the dress worn by the person who uttered the cheque is identically similar to one which Miss Treherne had made last year, and wore constantly." "Yes ? " she answered interrogatively. "I am quite certain that you can give me impor- tant information on one or two points. Will you tell me now who procured the cheque, who uttered it, and who forged it ? " With bewildering fear Grace looked at his decided face and noted his determined manner. . _ _

"Why do you—ask me—all—these questions ? " she stammered; "it is—" She ceased speaking abruptly, for her ears caught the distant sound of th) nurse singing her child off to sleep. The colour surged back into her cheeks, and she breathed quickly, as if labouring under intense excitement. "Dennis, I cannot tell you anything," she said with a tinge of decision. "In other words, you are determined to side with the true criminal, or criminals, and to participate in their dastardly conduct Do you not compre- hend the awful responsibility in which you are involving yourself—and others—by adopting this line of conduct, and by allowing a young, promis- ing life to be crushed—ground down with humilia- tion and degradation? By speaking up bravely and telling all you know, you will probably save her from this—and act a noble part. Fears of implicating yourself ought not to stand in the way of your doing what / tell you is right. Do not imagine, Grace, that you have married a man who would desert you in trouble. I will stand by you, through thick and thin, if you will act courageously and straightforwardly. I know you must regret doing many things which you were, in a measure, forced to do when living with your father. But if you will only be frank with me regarding all you know concerning this forged cheque, I will do my utmost to screen you from blame." As he ceased speaking, Grace raised her eyes to Mi. Oh! that at that moment they had reflected love! What sorrow would have been averted.

A painful silence ensued. In the distance the nurse could be heard still singing baby to sleep. The tender strain of the evening hymn which fell so soothingly on the unintelligent baby ears touched a pathetic cord in her mother's heart. She sighed and buried her face in her hands. Grief, contrition, and fear were coursing through her mind. To confess all, might it not force her to relinquish husband, child, and home? Her husband did not suspect the truth, or he would not have promised to screen her. The moment he knew all, he would turn from her in anger—per- haps decline to allow her to share his home or to see her child, her sweet little baby whom she loved so dearly. Why should such a sacrifice be expected from her now? She was so sorry for the past. Surely she had suffered enough for that one sin. Never to lie down or rise up without the haunting knowledge of that one dark spot on her life—was not that sufficient punishment? Besides, Richard Musgrove had told her not to trouble about the matter, that all would be right. He knew all the circumstances. Dennis knew nothing. It would be foolish not to follow Richard Musgrove's advice. As she thus argued to herself the vacillation which usually characterised her expression was suddenly replaced by obstinacy— that obstinacy which belongs essentially to the weak.

"Grace, how much longer must I wait for my answer ? " Dennis asked, somewhat imperiously. "I have no answer to give," she replied. "Tell me what you know of Richard Musgrove?" he asked in a desperate voice. There was no reply. Dennis pressed his lips together and grew deadly white.

"If you could only understand how I hate and despise myself—through you!" he exclaimed with bitter vehemence. "Cannot you see what a galling position mine is? But of course not!" he laughed unpleasantly; "your code of honour makes all things right in your eyes, probably. You evidently have not the slightest idea what pain it gives me to be compelled to provide the cat's-paw in this case. If you did, you could not remain silent; it would be a radical impossibility. Place yourself in my position; try and imagine my feelings, if I have to stand by and hear an innocent person con- demned to imprisonment and lifelong disgrace for forging my name. lam absolutely certain that she never uttered that forged cheque! You know it, too, and I believe it lies in your power to save her from imprisonment" He stopped speaking with startling abruptness. Grace raised her eyes and gazed searchingly into his face: acute emotion was there reflected. Sud- denly the knowledge that he loved Gwendoline Treherne flashed across her mind. With that knowledge came jealousy and hatred; for she knew it was Gwendoline Treherne who was holding back her hupband's love—that love for which she craved unceasingly. Until this moment she had been comparatively contented to be merely an unloved, though kindly-treated, wife. The hope that her husband would grow to love her ulti- mately had latterly assumed the aspect of a cer- tainty in her eyes. During the time of enforced idleness the thought of it had been seldom absent from her mind. Alas! those castles in the air, in which his love for her had played an all- important part, were now razed to the ground. Then it suddenly occurred to her that Dennis was anxious to sacrifice her—the mother of his child—in order to save her rival from imprisonment. She rose and walked with faltering steps across the room. At the door she stopped, and, turning round, said in a hard, deep voice: "Dennis, I know now why you want me to give you information about that cheque." "Do you ? " he queried unsuspiciously. "Yes; you love Gwendoline Treherne! You would be glad if I were sent to prison instead of her!

a lie, Grace--Stop! " he cried in a voice of thunder.

But the closed the door swiftly, and he was left alone.

THE WHEEL OF FATE.

BY MRS. BAGOT HARM. Author of "Love's Revenge," "Bianca," "The

Silver Lining," &c., &e.

===== 0002601_18980415_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XXIX

The Vanes' pretty drawing-room at Campden Hill Was laden with the perfume of roses and mignonette. The flowers had been sent from the country by Mr. Hughes as a congratulatory offering to Grace on , her recovery. "I never knew William to act so thoughtfully before," said Mrs. Hughes in a voice of genuine approval. "You must consider it a great compli- ment to yourself, dear Grace. I am quite sure he packed them himself—and in cotton wool, too! No one else would have fixed them in as if they were never intended to come out. William always does everything thoroughly—never by halves. He is a good, sterling character. Of course, women are very pleasant in their way, but—"— here Mrs. Hughes made a long pause, then added with an emphatic nod—"l am a great admirer of men." Grace glanced up with a look of surprise. "Yes, very great admirer of men," continued her aunt. "They may not be perfect; I have strong suspicions that many are very far from perfect; yet, as to whole, they are brave, large-hearted, persevering, and healthily. active. Ido not see why women should grumble about their men-folk so much as they do. lam snre that the way in which most men go cheerfully through their day's work, making light of their labours, is most creditable. Women, as a rule, have a bad habit of not doing cheerfully what they have to do. They complain about bein,, tired and not feeling well and all that kind of thing. I have a great idea that people should try to be cheerful—it does not matter how busy they are. Don't you agree with me, Grace ? " "Yes, certainly I do. But it is not always easy to be cheerful."

"I do not find it difficult. It is just a habit— that is all. It only requires people to make no their minds to look pleasant, and to think pleasantly, and—well, the thing is done! But to return to men. I don't believe in half the dis- agreeable things that are said of them. Of course, I know they are foolish and weak on some subjects, especially on women; and it always seems very thoughtless for a strong man to let his wife, perhaps a delicate, frail creature, carry a heavy baby for miles, when he walks at her side smoking. He doesn't like to offer to carry the baby—because he is a man! Then, it's very stupid that boys are taught that it isn't right for them to lend a hand in the house sometimes. I'm sure there's many a chivalrous-hearted little fellow who would help his mother if it were not for the thought that he mustn't—because he is going to be a man! But I daresay these things will be righted some day." Then, suddenly changing the topic of the conver- sation as was her wont, Mrs. Hughes made a series of disconnected comments on Dennis's goodness, to which Grace listened with a pleasant sense of happiness, as she leaned back placidly watching Mrs. Hughes arranging the flowers. On Grace's lap lay a mass of white muslin and lace, among which a wee face nestled, and two little pink fists were pressed together on a small chest. Baby slept calmly. Sometimes a sweet smile flitted over the little face, as if angels were whispering happy thoughts into her ears as she lay on her mother's lap. Grace glanced down admir- ingly at her child. What a pretty, Niree creature she was! How greatly she resembled her father. "We haven't chosen baby's name yet," said Grace smiling, as if selecting her baby's name was an interesting occupation. "Oh, no! so you have not , answered Mrs. Hughes. "Why not call her Arabella ? There's such a good, sensible sound about it; and she had an aunt of that name. By the bye, she was rather an eccentric person. She had some property near Liverpool, which a line of steamers wanted to buy from her. She said she would not sell it for cash, but, as she was fond of travelling about, she offered to exchange it for the right of travelling on their steamers whenever she liked. She got her way, and almost lived on board the company's steamers until she died." "I should not like to call my child after her," said Grace. "Then call her Grace Dennina, after her father and yourself," answered Mrs. Hughes in a practical voice; "and perhaps, as I love the sweet little creature as much as if she were my own grand- child, you could add my name. Dennina Grace Maria Vane would be a characteristic name—one that nobody could forget—and--" she stc'PPed abruptly and opened the door, then exclaimed in a joyful voice: "Yes, I thought it must be you! You dear boy Come in Now kiss me, and then go and kiss your wife and baby. But don't make any noise; speak low, and walk on tip-toe, for baby is asleep. We were just talking about what names she should be christened. I suggest Grace Dennina Maria." "Sounds like Grace Dinner Mercy. I'm afraid I do not admire the combination of sound—it's not euphonious," replied Dennis; then he added gravely: "I should like her to be called Phyllis— after my mother." "Of course—yes ' exclaimed his aunt. "How thoughtful of you to think of it. Dear Phyllis. It is only natural that her boy should want his child to be called after her. But draw your chair near, Dennis, and look at your child." Mrs. Hughes spoke as if the baby was a unique specimen of the genus homo, and as if she possessed rare and wonderful characteristics which demanded special study. "Just notice," she continued, adoptim, the manner of a show woman, "her sweet little ands —and her long eyelashes—and her broad, high forehead—and her beautifully curved mouth—and her nose--"

"I should describe that nose as a negative one," interrupted Dennis. "But I wonder who she will be like. At the present moment she resembles nobody." "Oh, Dennis! She is just like you! " expostu, lated his wife. "Like roe! I don't see where the resemblance lies, I must say. I should be surprised to hear that my no was that shape--" "It's a very pretty nose! " put in Mrs. Hughes, indignantly. "I won't contradict you, aunt," he said. Then, as baby opened her eyes and gazed straight at him, he added, with a ring of dismay, "It's waking up!" "It ! " cried Mrs. Hughes with a gasp of horrified surprise. "Pray never let ins hear you call the dear child 'it' again. It is a she." "Oh! it is a she, is it? " he observed with undisguised amusement. Grace smiled and looked happy; she enjoyed hearing her child discussed, and seeing her husband take an amused interest in his aunt's various remarks.

"I should dearly love to see you nurse your child, Dennis," said Mrs. Hughes, adopting a per- suasive tone. "It looks so charming to see a young father nursing his first baby.'' Dennis glanced hesitatingly at the mass of lace and muslin, and then, with the air of doing an heroic deed, extended his hands, saying: "How do you take hold of it?" "‘ It' again," echoed his aunt sorrowfully. But there NM a ring of forgiveness in her voice; for Dennis, contrary to all expectations, was hold- ing the baby most successfully. "One would imagine she was a giantess, judging from the length of her garments," he observed. "She will wear shorter ones by - and - bye," answered his aunt in an instructive voice. "Let us hope so. But why she should wear them three feet too long now baffles my comprehen- sion."

"It is the fashion," Mrs. Hughes explained. "Then if it were the fashion for babies to wear knickerbockers and swallowtail coats, she would have to wear them, I suppose, aunt ? " "I really cannot exactly say; but I should not imagine that such an extraordinary style of costume will ever be the fashion for infants." "Shall I take baby again, Dennis ? " said Grace. "Yes, do!'" put in Mrs. Hughes. "Come, give up tho sweet treasure, Dennis; you hsve had her quite long enough. I will ring the bell and send her up to the nursery. lam going out now to do some shopping. So you young peole can enjoy a quiet chat together."

===== 0002601_18980415_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
CITA?ThR XXX.

More effectual means for checking a tete-a-tite could not be chosen than telling the two people concerned to enjoy a quiet chat. Thus, as the door closed on Mrs. Hughes, neither Dennis nor Grace shtuied a disposition to start a conversation. The latter settled himself in his favourite chair and drew it close to an nrPn window. 'lron won't mind My havtng a cigarette here, •will you, Grace?" he asked. "Oh, no," she answered in a manner that re- vealed it was a pleasufe to acquiesce in whatever he suggested.

CHAPTER XXXI.

"OM William, I am so very glad you have come Now I shall have someone to talk to." Had Mrs. Hughes said—a sympathetic listener to r horn she could unburden her heart, she wou.4, hays' spoken more truthfully.

CJ am glad I have come, as you seem so pleased," concurred her husband. "But you haven't yet told me what the trouble is about which has upset you." 2 "Oh! so I have not. But do eat a good meal, Cold, chicken is really very nourishing, and Fon look as if you wanted keeping up. I'm afraid you have been vezi,Js badly looked after at home. Well, to return to poor Grace. I left her and Dertnis htiving a pleasant little chat yesterday "afternoon: He had been nursing the baby, and we :bad, just settled what names she should be christened and whom she resembled. Really, it was quite a pretty sight to see the young parenta leaning over the little treasure, both looking so .proud and happy. I started off to do my -shopping, feeling quite sure that all would go on smoothly whilst I was out of the house. I suppose_l must have been away some two hours, for I couldn't see anything I liked until they showed me a green plaid dress, trimmed with black fringe—you'll see it to-morrow, it's being altered to fit me—and—" "But about Grace, my dear," interrupted her husband.

"Well, when I got home I found everyone running about—oh! such excitement; and I could hear the poor girl sobbing and crying in her oat room. It was dreadful ! Dennis had gone oat' here her voice fell almost to a whisper—"km afraid they had had a little difference. Of course, I hurried up to her, and she threw her arms round my neck and implored of me to take her and her baby to some safe place, quite away from London. She said she was so miserable, and a great many other things that lam quite sure she never meant. When she was in the middle of tell- ing me all her troubles, nurse came in, and said I must try and quiet Mrs. Vane' or send for Dr. BoltOn at once. I did my best, but of course it was very difficult, for dear Grace was so excited and unhappy. She said a great deal that I should not like to repeat—even to you! She actually said that Dennis wanted her to go to prison instead of Gwendoline Treherne. I told her that it was very silly to talk like that, but she clung to me and repeated it several times." "Ah, poor girl; she must be slightly unhinged —mentally," said Mr. Hughes in a sad voice. "And how did it all end ? " _ _

"eh, -the-clOci-o;-h-a-d-t-o be sent for, as I could not succeed in pacifying her. He came last night and again the first thing this morning. He evidently thinks she is in a critical condition, and says that the only means of saving her from a long illness is to take her away from London at once, and not to let her see her husband for quite three months—perhaps even for a year. Is it not dread- ful ? "

"Very—indeed I" replied Mr. Hughes witk feeling. "But what can I do, Maria? Why dia you telegraph for me?" "Because I felt I couldn't tell dear Dennis what the doctor says," she said, gulping down a sob; "and I'm not a coward—as you know, William— s° 1 sent for you to break the news to him." "But you can tell him a hundred times better than I can. Women manage those kind of .com- munications much better than men do." "Oh, but I feel as if I couldn't tell him. just' imagine how dreadful it will be for the poor boy Lo have his home broken up and the dear baby taken away from him, and—and—all his domestic happiness ruined, and Grace so fond of him, too." "Yes; I only wish he was as fond of her as she is of him: However, my dear, we must leave the matter in Higher Hands. I wonder what led her to state that she believes he is fond of Miss Treherne ? "

"I am sure I cannot say. Dear me, those Tre- hernes seem fated to upset everyone's happiness. I do wish you would try and induce Dennis not to have anything more to do with them, and.especially not to continue figuring so conspicuously on Miss Treherne's side during this disgraceful trial, for of course it is disgraceful for a well-educated young person to be connected with cashing a forged cheque." "It is certainly most unfortunate that Dennie's name was forged. I can quite understand his feeling a chivalrous kind of pity for a girl whom he considers innocent of the charge; and there's always danger in--" "If Miss Treherne had a sallow complexion: a squint, and was fifty years of age, inste,ul of being young and pretty," broke in Mrs. Hughes, "neither you nor he would be so sorry for her, nor so certain that she did not cae:, ; ;alit cheque. Men are men, and pretty girls are pretty girls "We will not dispute that point, my dear," was the mildly-spoken rejoinder. 'Besides—" "Hush ! " exclaimed his wife. "There's Dennis! I wonder why he has come back so early. It is only four o'clock. . - -

dontrary to her usual custom, Mrs. Hughes did not hurry out to welcome him, but remained quietly listening to his movements in the hall. She evidently dreaded the moment when he should enter the dining-room. Meanwhile Mr. Hughes handled his knife and fork in a nervous manner, as if oppressed with a sense of uncongenial respon- sibility. "Why, uncle, you here!" exclaimed Dennis, entering and speaking in a manner that conveyed a warm welcome.

"Yes, my boy; your aunt telegraphed for me." Dennis made no answer, but looked interroga- tively towards her. "I thought that perhaps your uncle would be able to advise you in your trouble," she said responsively. "I am glad you have come, uncle," was the rejoinder; then turning to Mrs. Hughes, Dennis added, in a lower voice: "Has Dr. Bolton been again? " "Yes."

"What does he say?" Mrs, Hughes looked furtively at her husband, who, however, steadily averted his face. "I should like to know at once what Dr. Bolton said," Dennis added anxiously. "He says, dear boy," Mrs. Hughes began in a distressed voice, "that you must let your wife leave town immediately, and that please don't think I am unkind for repeating it—that you and she had better not see much—at least, anything_.. of each other for a time. It has happened, as it often does, that her mind has become a little upset. She imagines strange things about you and--- "I understand," interrupted Dennis, thrusting his hands into his pockets and looking straight before him; "Dr. Bolton thinks that we had better separate, temporarily." "Well, I suppose that is what he really means. In the end everything will come quite right, of course. Your dear wife is sure to recover, and the little feeling of—of jealousy which is now troubling her is certain to subside." "I thought it would come to this," Dennis jerked out, almost unconsciously. "I am so sorry for you—so very sorry," ex- claimed his aunt with a big sob. Then she rose quickly, hurried towards him, and threw her arms round his neck. "If you like I will take care of them both—your wife and your baby," she con- tinued in a semi-articulate manner. "They will be happy at the Vicarage, and—and—oh, Dennis, don't look so broken-hearted; all will come right." "You are very kind, aunt," he said, gently disengaging himself; "no one could be kinder. Yes; all will come right, probably. Meanwhile, I shall be very glad for Grace and the baby to stay with you. I hope you will not find them an anxiety." "I shall enjoy taking care of them. But even if they were an immense anxiety, it would not influence me in the matter. Do you imagine " here Mrs. Hughes adopted a mildly indignant tone—"l should allow your wife and your child to be taken care of by strangers—certainly not! "It is always a pleasure for your aunt to do kindly acts for you and yours," said Mr. Hughes, speaking for the first time. For a few minutes no one spoke; then Dennis said, in a frigid voice: "I shall let the house furnished, and shall probably share Flume's rooms." "Could you not induce him to come and live here? " suggested Mrs. Hughes; "it seems a sad pity for you to break up your home before you have been in it a year, and to let strangers hu3rl your pretty furniture." "I shall be glad to turn my back on it a 11...- heartily glad, replied Dennis emphatically. "Do not trouble yourself so much about me, aunt. Everyone has more or less bad luck in life. I suppose lam having my bad luck now. Later I may have good luck, though it seems anything but probable, at the present moment. However, lamentations are useless. We all have to grow old to an accompaniment of pleasant and unpleasant Incidents."

"That's only too true, Dennis,' coincided his uncle, "for man is born to trouble as the sparks fly upward.'"

The death-rate in some districts in alartnirg. Even where the best possible conditions prevail, it ought to be known that laundry work is a severe tax on a girl's strength. A recent case ferreted out by the "Bath Herald," illustrates the fact. She was deadly pale, breath. less, and hardly able to stand, owing to her emaciated and ansemic condition. The constant inhaling of air charged with half-con- densed steam ; the inevitable beat of the ironing rooms and the transition from heat to cold on passing out afterwards had doubtless reduce 3 her vitality. The heat had affected her heart; her blood was impover shed; and there is no doubt that she was on the verea of consumption when taken to the hospital. No blaino attached to anyone. " WOMEN'S CHAT."

The German Empress,who is still very far from strew,: has been ordered mountain air, and it is expected she will take advantage of the Queen's offer of the use of Abergeldie Castle, very shortly. There is perhaps on finer or more invigorating air in all Scotland than that of Deeside, and much is hoped from the Empress's stay there. By the way the Empre a dresses miles better now than she used to do,and, it is said, wishes to incite all German women to de the same, which would be an ealellent thing. She has of late grown much slimmer, and looks better in consf quence. Considering the Dutch are not usually regarded as a musical people, the nee a that Queen Wilhelmina pos- sesses a great distaste for music,scarcely comes as a sur- prise. If however she is not a musician,like her father, King William 111., the young Queen pays some attention to other aesthetic tastes. She takes the greatest interest in painting and sculptule,and has herself a pretty talent for sketching. The picturesque, old-fashioned peasant costumes still exist in Holland, and Berne of them, such as those worn in Noord-Holland. Lynd-Holland, and Friesland, are really pretty. The headdresses more especially are quite original. In the three provinces I have just mentioned, a great many very wealthy farmers are liviu. The farmers' wives when in full dress have quite a fortune upon their beads. They wear a sort of golden skull cap at the back of their head, and over this a real lace cap, sometimes with a lace veil falling upon the back as low as the waist. The forehead is covered with a golden plate set with diamonds, while the most fantastic ear- rings are worn, generally old-fashioned diamond speci- mens. The bodices are very short, and the skirts unusually wide—not at the bottom, like our modern skirts, but round the hips. The skirr is made with gathered flounces, and in spite of their large hips, the women wear a sort of"bustle"under the skirt, in order to make it as volum- inous as possible. The aprons.which are large and long are made of beautiful silks, and satins. It is only just to the Dutch woman though to add, that,if she does not dress well, she does not dress extravagantly. She makes no exorbitant demands upon her hnsband's pocket for "pin money," but is as economical with her wardrobe as with her household.

An appreciation of British women writers by a Frenchman is indeed rare, and unexpected, but none the less pleasing and interesting. M. Edouard Rod, Pro- fessor of Comparative Literature at the University of Geneva has described George Eliot as the greatest English fiction writer of this century, and her genius he puts on the same level as that of the great Russian nevelist—Tolstoi. Students and admirers of Mrs. Hum- phry Ward will be pleased with what M. Bod says of 66 Robert Elsmere," and the distringuished French writer does not forget Olive Schreinei , whose " Story of an African Farm" and "Trooper Peter Halkett," have made such a powerful impression upon thinking men and women. He considers the last named book one of the greatest productions of the present generation. Mrs. Humphry Ward. or to use the novelist's maiden name, Mary Augusta Arnold., was born with a rich heri- tage of intellect and culture. She was the grand- daughter of Arnold of Rugby, and the niece of Matthew Arnold, but the religous strain in her writings and the disposition to grapple with metaphysical difficultiee came from her father, Mr. Thomas Arnold. Mrs. Ward pre- fere to avoid publicity, still ehe is an occasional guest at literary dinners. Her tall graceful figure and distin- guished though unassuming bearing, make her a marked figure, in any assembly. She talks with ease, and in a clear melodious voice which never rises to the height of argument. In public gatherings Mrs. Ward surrounds herself with an atmosphere of reserve but amongst her intimate friends, she displays a quiet wit and enjoys the gay as well as the grave. In society she avoids the " smart " sets, preferring the atmosphere of thought and culture: All the rare point laces, made with such care and pre- cision in the old days, are being produced in our go- ahead times by machinery. Sometimes a faithful copy is made, but as a rule we adapt the old patterns to our modern uses. The newest ground work, which resembles the cells of a honeycomb, appertains only to our modern days, and it figures now in black and white laces alike. In white it is singularly beautiful with the braid designs united by lace stitches. Speaking of old lace, there is at the present time in South Kensington Museum, (London) an altar frontal, value at £350. The work in this is represented by birds scrolls, and blossoms, with an outer border of similar devices in bolder forms. The lace is ,gros point de Vonise, or heavy raised lace,and must have taken a couaiderable time to work. Concerning the flower world,forget-Ine-nots are shown in profusion, and, mixed with the deeper tints of violet, promise to be well worn. The foxglove too, bids fair to be very fashionable, as the clusters are full, and exactly in the present fashion of wreaths. This flower is pro- duced in all colours, with a dark shaded centre. The three-quarter coats bad a brief season. Nobody liked them, they looked too double-skirtish, and they needed such very careful fitting below the waist, as few mantua-makers had skill or care to give them. The smart coat and skirt costume of the moment, is about as neat and purposeful as anything one can imagine. Its small sleeves, short flat basque and stern cut are quite one's ideal of the walking gown which a Britishwornan finds reaindispensable an item of her wardrobe. Many people there are who spend much time and thought over the arrangement of their furniture, bat give little or no attention to the framing and hanging of their pictures. as a general rule photographs ought to be delicately framed in oxidised silver, r light enamel, otherwise their delicate tones are killed. Pencil draw- ings, for the same reason should also be framed in pale colors, the richness of gold moulding detracting too much from the victure itself. Etchings, and pen-and- ink sketches may, on the contrary have far heavier frames, and for these nothing looks better than fluted oak or walnut. If water colors be low in tons, they ' should be lightly framed, in enamel or oxidised eilver,as the photographs, but if the coloring of the pictures is high, there is nothing more suitable than dull gold. For on paintings alone, is burnished gold really suitable, and even for these it should be sparingly ust,d. In hanging pictures, it is well to avoid the moat corn mon error of placing them too low. And moreover pic tunes should be grouped., not arranged in long straight , rows,or dotted evenly on each wall expanse,in the room. I They should be arranged in a pretty uneven group, I where the pictures are of the same kind,and the frames identical. Then a restful undecorated piece of wall should be left before placing another group. As to the means of hanging pictures, wire, not cord, should be used. An inexpensive way in which to stain floors is as fol- lows:—First sizethe parts to be etained,then dissolve two ounces of permanganate of potash in one pint of boiling water,add a pint of ala,,stir well, and it is ready for use. Brush over the Boards once or twice until the desired colour is obtained,and use a large brush. It will dry in a few minutes. Size again,and when quite dry eivei a coat a varnish. This is an exceediegly good and econ- omical stain. A capital plan whereby to use up scraps of pastry and cheese is 0 roll out the former very thiu,and line patty pans with it. Next grate the doer's?, and when there is one ounce mix it with half an ounce of warm butter,the yolks of two and the white of one egg, a little cayenne pepper, and onaei,e mustard. Put a teasooonful of this mixture into each patty pan, and bake in a steady oven. " MADGE."

Woods (vestry clerk).—The Chairman in a brief re- view of the past year, said they had special services in connection with the celebrntion of the Diamond Jubilee of the Queen. The event was duly marked by giving a treat to the Sunday and day school schelars. As a paro- chial memorial to mark the jubilee, they were trying to raise funds to build a vicarage. The Ecclesiastical Commissioners said they were unable to receive the application. He hoped, however, they would see their way to assist them during the coming spring. He was willing to make another applicatioa, and he hoped they would get together £7OO. It was necessary to put their shoulders together if they wanted to raise the money they needed, which was £1,500. The heating apparatus in the church was now in perfect condition. He was thankful to be able to say that they had worked har- moniously together, and also thankful for the help that had been given by Messrs. Naylor and Winter, the churchwardens. The absence of Mr. Winter was un- avoidable, as he was in America, and he had received a telegram from him saying that he had safely arrived in Boston after p pleasant voyage. The income for the year amounted to £lO9 odd, and the expenditure £los,which left a balance of more than £3 in hand.--The a-counts were passed.—The following were appointed officers for the ensuing year :—Vicar's arden, Mr. Owen ; peoplee warden, Mr. G. Armstrong ; sidesrnen, Messrs. Brother - ton and Harrison, in addition to those who were appoin- ted last year • and lay representatives, Quarter-Master Sergeant Taylor and Mr. F. Taylor.—The Vicar thanked those who had assisted him in the parish work diving the year.

===== 0002601_18980415_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
STRETTON.

The vestry meeting was held in the schoolroom on Raster Monday evening. The Vicar (the Rev. the Hon. C.F. Cross) presided. The Vicar nominated Mr. Thomas Ditchfield as his warden ; and on the motion of Mr. Hugh Darbyshire, seoonded by Mr. Peter Leather, Mr. Ralph Tickle was elected people's warden. Mr.Ditchfield appointed Mr. Rutter to act as his sideaman and Mr. Tickle appointed Mr. Renshaw. The church accounts for the past year were presented and passed (showing. a balance on the right side on the motion of Mr. Whit- ley, seconded hy Mr. Rutter. On the preposal of Mr. Ditchfield, seconded by Mr. Tickle, the vestry meeting resolved that a hearty vote of thanks be given to the Ladies' Committee for the pains and care they had taken in the adornment and enrichment of the Parish Church. Mr. J. Darbyshire proposed, and Mr. Rutter seconded, that a hearty vote of thanks be given to the church- wardens for their services during the past year, which was acknowledged by Mr. Ditchield and Mr. Tickle. Votes of thanks were also passed to the sidesmen and Thar. The usual dinner was held at the at and Lion ardor ng.

AND IS ALL THE CHIEF CITIES ;AND TOWNS IN THEnKINC/DO5l.

Im. some respects the northern Temperance kingdoms of Sweden and Norway I Reform.

may be justly described as the pioneers of the temperance movement. In those countries the now celebrated Gothenburg system of dealing with the liquor traffic had its origin and has had its most instructive trial. This system has now passed through its experimental stage, and it has in the opinion of almost all observer° been a decided success. It has not entirely abet" ished the use of intoxicating drinks—that was not what it aimed at—but it has undoubtedly greatly reduced the vice of drunkenness in both INorway and Sweden. In Norway, where the democracy rules in deed as well as in name,a trial lof a more extreme kind of temperance reform has been made more recently. This atest experiment really consists of the total abolition of the drink traffic in several centres of population. A correspondent of the "Times" has given a report of the results from his own point of view. So far as can be judged this correspondent treats his subject with a reasonable amount of impartiality. At all events it is clear that he is not biassed on the side of temperance, reform. Yet he completely justifies the Gothen- burg system of licensing. On the other hand, however, he makes up for this concession to the temperance side by the vigour of his con- demnation ef the legislative attempts to enforce total abstinence. It may be here explained that he deals entirely with Norway, of which country he claims to have had twenty-five years experience. His description of the good that has resulted from the establishment of the Gothenburg system will encourage temperance people to believe that some improvements may yet be affected in this country. Wien this- system was enforced the sale of spirits by individuals was abolished, and the traffic in that particular commodity was placed in the hands of a company whose object is not wholly of a conv- mercial character. This company is prevented by law from earning more than five per cent, on the sale of spirits. All profits in excess of that have to be distributed for the public benefit. In Sweden these profits are used for the reduction of local rates. In Norway they are rather differ- ently allocated, part being given to philanthropic and educational institutions, and part to tem• perance societies. In the town of Bergen, for instance, both the local Total Abstinence Society and the Good Tempters derive pecuniary benefit from the sale of spirits. Last year, however some changes were made in the method of distributing the profits. It was thought that the companies had too much money to dispose of over anti above their five per cent, and they wer partially disendovred. Sixty-five per cent of the profits were taken by the Government, 15 per cent. were handed over to the municipalities, and only 20 per cent left to; the companies to distri- bute. Now as to the result of the system frore the temperance point of view. According to the correspondent referred to, it has led to a verl great decrease of drunkenness. The rules for the sale of spirits are exceedingly strict and no person under eighteen years of age is per.nitted to enter the dramshops. Early closing and Sunday closing are enforced to an extent that is unknown else' where. But, we are further told, the temperance party has now gone farther and fared worse. TO total abstinence party is strong in Norway-1 country where the people are highly educated se' thoroughly accustomed to manage their own affair°

—and they have demanded and obtained a rigP of local option. It was enacted that in every tow') where a company existed the inhabitants—no and women—should have the right to say by Ote whether the sale of spirits should be put an end fA)

I.

altogether. The new system is being introduce gradually, but at the first elections in 1896, the abolitionists secured sweeping victories. The ecioapanies, or Samlags as they are called in Norway-, were abolished in sixteen out of twenty-two towns. Bet the results have, it is "ged, not answered the expectations of the abolitionists. In three of the largest towns whore the dram-shops have been entirely removed drunkenness has increased instead of diminished. This deterioration in public virtue is ascribed to two causes. The abolition of the legal sale of Spirits has—and in all this we are simply quoting the "Times" correspondent—led to the establish- tent of illicit stills from which people are supplied :With the potent intoxicant. It has also led to an Increased consumption of a kind of port wino known as " laddevin." This wine is "made in Germany" and seems to be extraordinarily effica- cious in producing intoxication. What is still 3:core deplorable, "laddevin" is said to have great attractions for the rising generation. Being a sweet liquor it appeals to the youthful taste, and the increase of drunkenness amongst the young is Said to be particularly noticeable. This then is the gist of the report of the "Times" correspond- ent. Temperance reformers will note with satis- faction the complete admission that restrictions tTon the liquor trade such as those imposed by the Gothenburg system have the effect of de_ Creasing drunkenness. That in itself is some- thing to the good, and should have some in- thience on the struggle for temperance reform Which is always proceeding in this country. The allegations against prohibition look, it is true, a.erious enough as they stAnd, but they need sift- tag. There may be, and doubtless is, something to be said on the other side which the " Times"

correspondent has not thought it worth while to Zention. Total abolitionists will no doubt be able and ready to defend their own cause, and 'When they have formulated their reply, the public • Itill be able to judge between the two parties. This much is clear. Restrictions upon the liquor trade of a fairly stringent character have de- cidedly good results, and few people will deny that there is room for large improvements in that respect in this country.

THE country is threatened with Another Little War, the outbreak of another little war, the scene of the disturbance 011 this occasion being Sierra Leone, tho oldest of Our West African colonies. Disorder and blood- shed seem to be rampart in that portion of the Empire at the present moment, and there is al- ready some talk of despatching an expeditionary force of white soldiers. Official incompetence and 'nal-administration are mentioned as the causes of the outbreak. The slaves of the big chiefs in the country at the back of the colony have been emancipated and they have been put into Uniform and endowed with the authority of Policemen. They have, as might have been ex- Noted, wretchedly betrayed the trust that was reposed in them. The unfortunate natives have been so atrociously treated that they have risen Ua rebellion and in some cases are leaving their Villages andseeking a refuge in French territory. Another act of official stupidity is the imposition of a hut tax, an impost against which the Sierra Leonean black man seems to have an almost su- perstitious objection. The consequences of all this bungling are deplorable. The very consider- able trade in which Liverpool and Manchester are ePecially interested is for the time being ruined. Ihe only remedy for these ills which the Colonial °ince at home appears to have yet thought about le the consignment of fifty tons of war material to Sierra Leone, but it is quite obvious that 15°Imething besides small arms ammunition is re- quired to meet this particular case.

"The Most Interesting House in Warrington" is the title of a pamphlet writtsn by Mr. H. Stuart Page, of Warrington ,and just issued by the Sunrise Publishing Company. The house referred to is of course the Old Academy, which far many years has been hidden from the public gaze by what has been larailiarly known as Marsh's shop in Bridge-st. The demolition of the shop premises has brought the 4Cademy once more to the front of the street, and " its fate still bangs in the balance,the issue of the P4rophlet may be considered most opportune. It is 'written with a local patriot's enthusiasm for the tiatoric house, and he pleads earnestly for its pre- servation. He passes in review the eminent persons 'Who have been associated with the Academy, and Proceeds to suggest a scheme for converting the Academy into a Local Literary Museum for the Preservation of our local literature. He says it blight contain the local books, which number over Vivo thousand, the local prints and portraits, and 4130 the literary relics, of which there are many in the Museum. As an alternative scheme it suggested that the building might be made .;":e headquarters of a society having for object the encouragement of every form of 46cal literary and artistic effort. Both schemes n4eiertl feasible, but, of course,the inevitable question 't Coat comes in. Is the Corporation prepared to 1:431 the cost of maintenance ? There cannot be the tehlUotest chance slit being made "to pay," but still benefits which might be derived are not to be Zessured in the Xs. d. scale. The monuments of totirtington's past are few in number, and they are "I:Melly becoming lees year by year, and we be- ar' 6 it would be both injudicious and show a lack Ruud taste if this building so rich in memories Should be placed under the hammer for the price of t b(lrid-hand building material.

Charming weather prevailed on Good Friday, and the pleasure seekers of Runcorn and district were not slow to take advantage of the opportunity that presented it- self for a day's outing. The services at the different places of worship were well attended. At the Parish Church, the Vicar (Rev. Canon Wood), occupied the pul- pit in the morning, the offertories of which were in aid of the Chester Diocesan House of Mercy. The usual three hours' service was held at St. Michgelmas Church. At St Edwards Catholic Church the ceremony of kissing the feet of the figure of Christ on the Cross was very solemnly carried out by the congregation. In the after- noon the statione of the Cross service was gone through, a large congregation being in attendance. The alter was devoid of evetything gay and had quite a solemn appear- ance. In connection with the Primitive Methodist Church a tea and entertainment was given in the schoolroom,the programme being well sustained by a number of friends: Eastertide was appropriately celebrated at the Parish Church, St. Michael's, Weston Church,and Holy Trinity. At St. Edward's the altar was beautifully decorated with Bowers. The Pastor, th 2 Rev. Father Gore, preached a sermon on the " Lord is risen." Special services were held at the Bethesda Congregational Chapel. The Pas- tor (the Rev. J. Osborne, B, AO preached an appropriate sermon, basing his realm ks on the lessons to be derived from a reverential interpretation of the solemn epoch which Eastertide marked in the religious life of the world, Mr, M, Gowanlock sang " Calvary," and Miss Edith Clara gave " I know that my Rec.etnaer liveth," On Easter Monday the atmeepheric conditions were de- cidedly against holiday-makers, but nevertheless a few did venture to go bevond the town of Runcorn. The town itself was quiet, all sign of business being entirely absent, -

SUDDEN DEATEI OF MR. EDWIN TtiCIZT. We regret to record the death of Mr. Edwin Tuckey, hay and corn merchant and canal carrier, of Percival- lane, Runcorn, which sad event took place on Monday night at his residence in Greenway-road. Mr. Tuckey had complained & few days ago of net being well, but I he took no eerieus notice of himself and went about his business as usual. On Sunday evening he went out for a walk, and the following day, Easter Monday, he stayed at home all day. He Wad attended to by the people of the house during the day, but it was not until ten o'clock at night that any serious developments were realised in his condition. Dr. McKenzie was immedi- ately sent for and on his arrival the latter ordered him to bed. At the doctor's request he went to bed, but be- fore doing so he remarked "that he did not think lie was as bad as all that," Dr. McKenzie then left the house to have a consultation with Dr. Ormsby, but before he returned Mr. Tuckey had passed away. Pneumonia was given as the cause of death. As soon as the news of the sad event became known regret was expressed by everyone as he was very highly respected for his upright character and generous and charitable disposition. Deceased gentleman was well known in Cheshire, Lancashire, Yorkshire, and the Midlands, where his business transactions extended. He was a bachelor and was 41 years of age, and was also a mem- be&ef the Ellesmere Lodge of Freemasons]. The re- mains of ueceased gentleman were interred in the Run. corn Cemetery on Thursday afternoon amid many mani- festations of regret. The cortege was headed by the following members of the Ellesmere Ledge of Free- masons :—Dr. McKenzie, Messrs. F. Findley, J.Higgins, Burrowe,Priestner,Eßll,Westbrooke,Groome,Hunaphreys, W. Griffiths, F. Ford, Captain Ellis, W. Miles, Lunt, Hoxwortb, Hayes, H. Hughes, F. Auderton, P. Riley, Captain H. Hill,, T. Harrison, J. Okell, S. Littler, G. Hewitt, Hicks, J.Jones, G. Sornerville,Burgess,Price, Philips, Branch' Collier,Newton,R.R.Clark,Dunning, and F. H. Annett. The chief mourners were R. Tuckey, Elijah Tuckey, Elisha Tuckey (brothers). The Rev. W, &boles performed the burial service.

He bad not been able to attend very well for the last 12 months, and he felt that he had not done his duty. Be had only done his duty as a member of the Bend, and he was glad to find that his fellow mem- bers entertained such kindly feelings towards him. Mr. Williams said he regretted that he was compelled to leave the Board, His services had been given without stint, and he had had no object to serve,but that of the public interest. His work had always been a source of interest as well as pleasure. Why his services were not appreciated he must leave them and other people to judge.—Mr.Melntyre observed that it was one of the in- cidents of war.—Mr.Williame said during hia experience of that Board he remembered nothing, but that which had been of a gentlemanly and respectable character,and it was with great regret that he severed his connection with them. He thanked them all for their kindly feel- ing towards him. This was all the businons.

THE WARRINGTON INDUSTRIAL EXHIBITION.

PRESEN ELTION'IO MR. HOOFER.

The Warrington Trades and Industrial Exhibition was brought to a close on Tuesday evening after 12 days of almost unparralelled success. It was opened by the Mayor on March 30th, since when the public have flocked to the Parr Hall in their thousands. Mr. W. J. Hooper has been responsible for the successful organisa- tion, and his abilities were fittingly recognised on Tues- day evening, in the presence of a crowded assembly. About 9 ocleck the Mayor (Alderman J. E. Wright), who was accompanied by the Mayoress, Mrs. Hooper, and others, toek up a position on the platform, and said that the first part of his duty was to present to Mrs. Hooper a beautiful bouquet, which had been subscubed for by the exhibitors. (Applause.) Ile had the honour of opening the exhibltion,which had been the most suc- cessful ever held in the tows; and now he was presiding : over a ceremony at the close. He had always beeps proud of his town,but he was prouder of it that day than ever, net simply because he happened to be the Mayor of it, but because he saw it making such wonderful progress, and because, apparently, every one was happy. The exhibition had been carried out with great credit to Mr. Ilooper.and they would always be glad to see him in Warrington to assist them in a similar work. He hoped the day was net far distant when they would have another exhibition but on a larger scale. (Applause.) I Mr. WILLIAM ROBERTS said he had a pleasing duty to perform, and why he had been selected for the task he knew not, except that he happened to be one of the smallest exhibitors. The people of Warrington would have been dissatisfied had the exhibition closed witheut acknowledging their debt of gratitude to Mr. Hooper. ' He supposed it was the first exhibition of the kind they had been favoured with in Warrington, and it was a re- markable fact that that magnificent building hail been too small for the purpose. He had been asked to pre- sent an illuminated address to Mr. Hooper on behalf of the subscribers, congratulating him upon the success of the exhibition, and wishing him every success in similar undertakings in .:he future. That was the first exhibi- tion, but he hoped it would not be the last. A very large number of manufacturers and tradesmen would gladly have exhibited had space allowed, but when th4y got the hall extended over the adjacent area they might be able to do something on a larger scale another year. (Applause.) Mr. Roberts then formally made the pre- eenAtion. _

IThe text of the address which had been framed by Mr. Jordan, Bridge-street, is as follows :—"To Mr. William James Hooper, gene-al manager. The exhibitors in the Warrington Industrial Exhibition, held in Parr Hall from March 30ch to April 12th, 1893, wish to express their entire satisfaction with the able manner in which you have designed and carried out the above Exhibition. You will no doubt be pleased with the manner in whites your scheme was received and the remarkable SLICCOSII which has resulted from your carefully-planned arrange- ments and effective supervision. We have great pleasure in acknowledging the courtesy shcwn by you, accom• ponied by the most careful attention to the requirements of the exhibitors, and the large number of visitors who attended the Exhibition. Accept our heartiest wishes that every success may attend your efforts here and else- where in carrying out similar exhibitions,—We remain, kc. (signed en behalf of the exhibitors)." Mr. HOOFER, in reply, said that when he came to Warrington abeut three months ago he was entirely un- known here. He did not even know where Parr Hall was, but It was very gratifying to him to find that be had made so many triends, and he hoped few, if any, enemies. (Applause.) He came from Birmingham,which was supposed te be the best governed city in England, but when he went,back be should tell the people that he knew one place as good, if not better. (Laughter and applause). He should tell the Chief Constable who was a personal friend of his, that be had held an exhibition in Warrington which ha i been visited by MOW people in 12 days, and he could say without fear of contradic- tion that they had not had a solitary case of theft or disorder, or a bad wird from a man or woman. (Ap- plause.) They bad had found in the building several larva sums of moner, including a bank book containing £157, A purse containing £7 125., and any number of umbrellas and parasols, that all had been returned to the rightful owners. (Applause.) He was not going to take the credit of the exhibition all to himself. He was deeply indebted to the Town Clera, who had been of great aofistance to him,and he had also to thank the exhibitors for their splendid displays, the Ladies Orchestra, and the Mayor and Mayoress for their pres- ence now and when the exhibition was opened. When he first came to the town the first thing that was handed to him was the report of the Chamber of Commerce, which he read carefully, and it struck him that the Chamber was a wide-awake oue in asking the Corpora- tion to have an exhibition. And nearly all the members of the Chamber were exhibitors that day. Mr. Charn- berlain a far-seeing man, got together through the aid of foreign consuls, a very large collection of samples of foreign manufactured goods, which had been shown in Warriogton, and throughout The Baited Kingdom. so that it could be seen what Englishmen had to compete against. No less a personage than the Prince of Wales, who was the head of the Royal Commission in connec- tion with the Paris exhibition, had made a valuable sug- gestion, and it was that instead of each country having a section to itself, each industry in all the countries should be placed side by side, so that Ragland would be able to hold her own against all contpetitors.(Applause.) He was sure that if Warrington manufacturers took part in that exhibition the industries of the town would bear favovrable comparison with those of any part of the world. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) In conclusion Mr. Hooper said that if the exhibition had not been a success, he should have put his hands in his pocket and paid up like a man. He had promised £5O to the Nursing Association—a most deserving charity—and he was sorry he was not able to do more to assist it ; but if he had the pleasure of com- ing amongst them again they might rest assured that he would do still more for the charities of the town. (Haar, hear.) He thanked all the visitors for their attendance. He had made many friends,and if he had unknowingly offended anyone he hoped be would be forgiven. (Ap- pla use.) Councillor BENNETT, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mayoress, said that, as he predicted a fortnight ago, the exhibitien had been a great success. His only regret was that the exhibition, instead of being under the patronage of the Corporation, bad not been promoted by it, for Mr. Hooper had shown them such possibilities that they now regretted they were not bold enough to take up the matter themselves. In a larger building they could have better exemplified the numer- ous industries of the town, industriee, many of them, of world-wide reputation, and—he was just reminded by Mr. Birtles—they night also have had a representative collection of the arts of the good old town, for Warring- ton was a town of many industries, a town which was proud of such men as Luke /tildes, George Sheffield, Warrington Wood, and Harry Woods. (Applause.) Mr. T. E. Guasoor seconded the proposition, and said he thought they might look upon the exhibition as an- other sign of the progress of Warrington. It was most pleasing to bear witnese to the remarks which had been made by Mr. Hooper as to the manner in which the .people of Warrington had assisted him to carry out the Exhibition, arid he (Mr. Gibson) hoped that that would be the first of a series of exhibitions, each one larger and better than its predecessor, and that the people of War- rington would join together—these who had capital and thome who had skill—each one sinking private feeling for the public good and progress of the town. (Applause.) TM motion was heartily agreed to. The MAYOR responded, and wished Mr. and Mrs. Hooper" God speed "in their future undertakings. The Mayorese, lie could assure them, was anxious to do all she could for the benefit of the town, and be believed that before their term of office expired the people of Warrington would be satisfied with what bad been done. (Applause.) Handsome medals have been presented to moat of the exhibitors, and it may also be interesting to note that time handsome cot shown by the Mersey Bedstead Co, Limited, Knutsford-road, has been purchased from that firm (through Messrs. Pendlebury and Co.) by the Mayor. _......_

The treasurer's report in the absence of the treasurer was submitted by Mr. J. A. Hayes. This was also a very satisfactory report, the whole of the liabilities of the club amounting to over £6 having been met, and a email balance left in hand .—The Chairman in remarking on the reports said that evidently the club was in a good p,,sitioti, and this was due he thought to the enthosiasm of the members. Be was glad to netice the membership had not decreased, which was very satisfactory ind ed. Some members had had to sever their connection with the club having left the town, and others had found their tastes did not lie in the direction of a literary society, but this loss had been coinpepstted by the joining of new members. Smith stated that whilst he was on duty in Battersby- lane, shortly after 12 o'clock, be heard a noise of break- ing glass. He then went into hiding near the shop, and a few minutes afterwards he saw the prisoner come across the road, from an entry, where he had been hiding, and he arrested him. Prisoner became violent and threw a tin of salmon at him, but just missed him. He then get him down and put the snaps on and took him to the Police Station, where the goods mentioned in the charge were found in his possession.—Mr. Rudd gave evidence to the effect that hearing a noise he got up, and when he got to the door he found the window broken and the prisoner in custody.
