EMMANUEL CHURCH INSTITUTE, WARRINUTON.

"THE REFORMERS OF THE lies CENTORY." The Rev. C. J. Millar cencluded his series of fed- nightly lectures on" The Reformers," by taking" John Wesley" as his subject on Thursday last week. There was a good attendance in the schoolroom over Arpley station, and much interest was taken in the lecture, which, in common with the whole series, has been of an attractive and instructive character. At the outset the lecturer said that as we associated Wycliffe with the rectory of Lutterworth so the cradle of the Wesleyans was to be found in another little country rectory,namely, Epwerth in Lineolnshire. He then went on to describe the condition of England in the 18th century, dwelling particularly on the Vale of Cheddar in Somersetehire as described by Hannah More. She alluded to the farmers of that fertile vale as being "as ignorant as tile beasts that perish," and added that "every day before dinner she could plunge into such vice as to make her think London a virtuous place." The star that arose in this place of darkness was John Wesley. He had been or lained a clergyman of the Church of England in 1725 and together with four young Oxford students began to spend some evenings in the week in studying the Greek Testament. They partook of the Holy Communion weakly and had regular days of fasting and prayer,and it was thraugh this very regularity that they obtained the name of Methodists. In fact Methodism during its early history was a Society of English Churchmen banded together for mutual edification, for prayer, and for the study of the Scrip- tures. Wesley would hold no service nor would he suffer any of his preachers to hold a service in any place at the same time as the Church service, and the first Methodists held their services on an early hour in the morning or late at night in their Parish Churches. Perhaps somebody might ask "if John Wesley never wished his Society to leave the Church of England why did he form any Society at all ? " The answer to that question must be found in the works of Macaulay and Fronde, and in the picture that they drew of the social and religious life in the days of Queen Anne, and of the Georges. Wssleyans, said the lecturer, had a forgotten gospel to preach,--the Goepel of convenion by which men were made new creatures. Though the churches were soon shut against him, and his zeal and theory of instan- taneous conversion offended the ministers of the Episco- pal Church, yet John Wesley now preached in the open air. His church was no longer bounded by four walls and a roof, but by the vault of Heaven above, and by the range of his powerful voice below. Never man preached before or since, as John Wesley preached, and a writer who was contemporary with him said that " men were smitten by his words as a field of standing corn by the tempest." Wesley could not remain in the Church of England with men whose teaching and funda- mentals of religion were diametrically opposed. The Methodists elected to leave, and no reconciliation, was, as far as the lecturer could see, possible. At the close a collection was taken towards liquidat- ing the debt on the church, and Mr. Miller announced that he would shortly deliver a popular lectura on " Scottish Wit and Humour."

DO YOU DESIRE LONG LIFE?

In Deuteronomy xxxiv. 7, we read that " Moses was an hundred and twenty years old when he died; his eye was not dim nor his natural strength abated." And in- stances of extreme longevity appear often is the news- papers of our time. Russian statistics vouch for the ex istenee at the present day of several persons who have exeeeded a century,net only by thirty or forty,but even by fifty years. In a late number of the " Nineteenth Century" Lady Glenesk argues that tha human being was intended for greater length of life than is usually attained in our artificial existence ; and that, under fa- vourable oircumstanceg, he ought to lire about one hun- dred and twenty years. It is a well-attested scientific fact that an animal lives from five to eight times as long as the period of its growth.

Now, when the ripening period is not artificially has- tened, man requires at least twenty-five years for the organs of the body—not counting the brain, which de- velops later—to reach full growth and maturity. IF, therefore, the ratio just named applies to him (and why should it not ?) he should reach an age quite equal to that of Mosse, And cases are on record which juitify this presumption. Among modern English examples of longevity may be mentioned Lady Smith, who lived to be one hundred and six ; Miss El'z %both Gray ( an aunt of General Cunningham Roberts), will died in 1853 within twenty-two days of her one hundred and eighth birthday ; MIA Johanna Hastings, of Malvern, wlio hi 1835 attained her one hundred and third oirthday ; Wil- iam Mann,of Kirkeeld,near Hadde,whe died at one hun- dred and stem, and Sir Mists Mmteft3re, who died in 1815 in his one handre 1 and fir3t year. Tress am 3b 33 a few out of very many well-attested cases. Tnt ql33ti)a then a.ri334 Why Sc no Xt3DiiOil anm not the rule. What prolongs the lives of a fee to the natural limit, and what shortens the lives of the great majority ? Is it special Providence ? No ; Providence has no favourites . It is unnecessary disease—disease which a larger wisdom may show us how to avoid, or to Cure at its very beginning. • _ .

Here speaks one of the multitude whose lives ought to be loegsr and more efficient.

"In February, 1591," he says, " after al attack of I influenza I was feeble and Jepressed. I had a foul taste in the mouth and was constantly spitting up a thick phlegm—or mucus. I had no relish for food of any kind. Even when I took a little light nourishment, to keep life in me, it seemed to do m3re harm than good. It gave me pain at the p it of the stomach, with distress at the chest. Then a nasty cough set in, which troubled me day and night. I had heavy nightsweats, and was so weak and done for that I gave up all hope of getting better. I feared I was going into a consump tion. I lost all my strength, and if I walked out I had to rest now and then lest I should fall in the street. No medicine that I took helped me. "While I was in this miserable state a little book came into our house telling of cases like mine being cared by Mother Seigel's Syrup. My wife urged me to give it a trial. I consented, and she g,t me a bottle from Mr. W. Sherrat, ahernist, in Q•leen's Road. When I had taken that bottle my appe'ita was bettor, and light food agreed with ms. I kept on with it and soon the cough and night sweats ceased. and I gained erength every day. But I had sunk s 3 low that it took a little time to set me again on my feet. However, when I had used six bottles I was healthy ancrstrong as ever, and able to resume my business. Since then I have been in the best of health. I tell everyone that your medicine has wrought a miracle in my ea an. (Signed) Joshua Lee, 3 Sutherland-street,Queen's-road, Miles Platting, Manches- ter, June 25th, 189 g. One-seventh of the human race die of consumption, and a larger proportion of dysp3psia and the organic diseaees which arise directly from it. Life is the result of the d;gestion of food, and illness and death of the allure of that function. Without good digestion you can never b 3 well,or can you hope for long life. People who itve to be a hundred taus t be able to recruit their strength constantly by means of thoroughly digested food. In all the wide world there is no substitute for this process, nor will there ever be. The increase of longevity depends on it as heat and light depend on the sun in heaven. And to promote perfect digestion there pi nothing to compare with Mother Seiget's Syrup. The secret of long life seems to be in it.

THE WA.RRINGTON WORKS COMPETITION.

CASTLE RUBBER WORKS T. PEARSON AND KNOWLES. Played on Monday. J. Woodward commenced for the Ironworkers. Spruce and Chapman participated in some clever work for the Rubber Works, Burgess scoring a fine try, Chapraan turning it to good account. Forster made an effort to get clear from a scrimmage, but was grassed before he could part with the ball. There seems to be a belief, amounting to conviction, in the United States, that the Mormon Chul oh was not honest in its a bargionment of polygamy. but simply prof.'ssed its All' htion to secure Stateh,,nd. The difficulty of gettino trultworthy statistics is almost insuperable, As all Mormon marriages are performed in Mormon temples, when Gentiles are never allowed to enter. All ceremonies are secret, and all records hidden. But the " Independent," a New York religious paper, sent a circular to the postmasters of Utah asking certain speci- fic questions. The replies show that 2,500 men and 5,500 women were living in polygamy at the time of Utah's admission to the Union, and that at the present time " nearly all" have resumed their former relation.

The German Government has resolved not to intro- duce a new rifla of smaTer calibre than the present one, as it is more easily deranged and not 'note serviceable in other resmcts. The preeent rifle, however, is to be furnished with a strip of steelplate instead of the cart- ridge-box, to facilitate loading., and a wooden case for the barrel, instead of the mantle. The method of clean- ing has been cmsidvrably improysd, the rifles having suffered materially from the present method. These im- provements will be expensive,but not nearly co much so as the introduction of a new weapon. A lighter and smaller side atm, better adapted for fixing to the rifle, will probably be introduced.

If the news that has come simultaneously from Vic- toria and oattle be true, a great disaster has happened on the road to Klondike. A body of emigrants seem to have been in the act of extricating their baggage from a snew-slip when a second and larger mass of snow fell down and buried them. The number of lives lost is estimated at from fifty to a hundred or more. Appar- ently it i 4 not known at al accurately how many travel- lers were buried in the snow, but olly twenty-five Were taken out alive, and twenty-two dead bodies had been recovered when the message left. Sheep Camp is the first of the two regular stopping places (m the weary road up the western side of the Chiikoot or Taiya Pass. Beyond it lies the most diffi,mlt part of the ascent, and there is a great glacier just above it. The snow lies thick all round, in the pass as well as on the slopes above ; indeed it is the snow under foot that makes the Chilkoet PASS in winter the best of the routes to Dawson City. When the summer thaw sets in.the track be- comes impassable far pack animals, and we know now that even at the beginning of April the snow may be. come a serious danger instead of a help t 3 the traveller. r This disaster shows the urgent need for the construction of a safe road for the vast stream of emigrants to the goldfields.

The latest papers from Ausiralia contain a god many comments on the behaviour of the spectators of the re- cent cricket matches. Several members of Mr. SW- dart's Eleven appear to think that there was a deliberate intention to insult them,and in one of his later speeches Mr. Stoddart publicly complained that they had been " hooted and hissed in every match and on every guaind without exception." The general Australian opinion,on the other hand,is that the visitors have taken the matter too much to heart, if indeed they have not often cursed the, A.ustralian habit of " barracking " with personal bitterness. The visitors might says the " Manchester Guardian," et least take comfort from the reflection that tha " barracking " was distributed ; no one was more unmercifully chaffed than Mr.Gs.rrett in one of the matches on the Sydney ground, and he is an Australian of Australians. No doubt the behaviour of spectators was often too truculent to be re- garded as a joke,but there are boors and silly people in every crowd, and it would be a pity if no allowance could be made for these things by men who come from a country where referees sometimes leave the playing-field on a shutter. Ranjitsinhji is very naturally less quali- fied to understand the c ,lonial manner than other mem- bers of the team,and one is not surprised to learn that he is peculiarly aggrieved. If the " barracking " inter- feres with the cricket as talking " on the stroke" inter- feres with the playing of golf,then the mauagers of clubs have a fair excuse for abating the nuisance without offending anybody.

The " Times " Vienna correspondent telegraphs Some interesting particulars of a new application of the Rentgen rays for curative purposes were communicated by Dr. Edward Schiff,lecturer at the Vienna University, at the last sitting of the Imperial and Royal Medical S:)- cietv. A series of experiments conducted by Dr. Schiff and his assistant proved that these rays could be used for the cure of disease in a manner capable of perfect control by means of a more or less intense application for a longer or shorter period,producing reaction in the exact degree required. In this way it has been possible for the lecturer, on the one hand, to remove hair from parts of the body where it constituted a disfigurement without causing the slightest infiammation,while,on the other hand,he has been able to treat lupus with uniform succeol by means of an artifi3ial inflammation the inten- sity of which he was in a position to increase or reduce at will. The results sscured by the new method both in the removal of superfluous hair and the treatment of lupus were demonstrated in the persons of some of Dr. Schiff's patients.

The recent week of self-denial observed by the mem- bers of the Salvation Army has yielded in the British Isles alone £33,000, or nearly £B,OOO more than was similarly raised last year. The returns from British colonies and foreign c untries have not yet been re- ceived. Wales sends £1,344, Manchester, £1,105 Tele- graphing from the United States, General Booth con- gratulates his " British forces " upon the results of their self-denial efforto. no adds :—" Your magnificent and unprecedented self-denial achievements have earned my deepest gratitude. lam satisfied that the outcome of the offering will not only prove of immense benefit to the dark, suffering, sinning masses, but also constitutes a monumental object-lesson to the entire Army."

In spite of his eighty years King Christian of Danmark may often be seen in tha streets of Copenhagen walking about with steps so light and elastic that he may be envied by many men not half his age, or he may be seen on horseback, erect as a young lieutenant who has just left his military school. When King Christian was in London five years ago at the wedding of the Duke of York he was one day seen on the top of an omnibus, which he had mounted in order to get a better view of London street life, and nobody who saw him jump into the rail way carriage at Liverpool-street Station on the day he left could think of a man in the middle of his eighth decade. The King has not grown much older since then.

A cow is the last creature one would expect to 8003 with earringq,yet every cow in Belgium must wear them now. The director-general of agriculture has issued a regulation that all animals of the bovine species are to wear earrings as soon as they have attained the age of thve months. This is a hygienic measure, intended to prevent the introduction into Bolgium of animals suffer- ing from tuberculosis. Breeders are obliged to keep an exact account of the animals raised by them, and the ring, on which is engraved a number, is fastened in the animal's ear for the purpose of preventieg the substitu- tion of one animal for another.

The potato has the following composition in parts per 100 : Watsr, 75 77. Albuminoids, 1 79. Fat, .16. Carbohydrates, 21.31. Salts, '97. Here the nutriment may be looked upon as about 23 per cent; but it should be noted that the albuminous matter surrounds the skin of the potato,and if the skiu is pared off one cannot help but remove this albuminous matter at the same time; 'tiler( fore, potatoes should be boiled in their skins, which makes it nossible to remove them without the albumin- ous layer underneath. Moreover, the skin acts ask mem- brane resisting the soaking out of the valuable salts. If potatoes are peeled they should not be allowed to soak in water before boiling, as this helps to dissolve out the soluble salts. The dissolving out of thr salts is reduced to a minimum by baking or steaming the potatoes.

Mr. Consul Cusack Smith, in hie repor . of the trade of Samoa issued on Wednesday, sheds tears of mingled ad- miration aria envy over the enterprise of the unwearied Teuton. "A high•class German commercial traveller," he sacs, "has recently visited Samoa with excellent samples at really rnarvellously chsap prices, and ob- tained £2,000 worth of orders, mainly from British traders. Be will be three years absent from Germany, and is now visiting the coloniee. It each colony there awaits him a completely new and different set of samples suited to that particular colony, and be antici- ipates a very large business." The curious thing is that this traveller admitted that most or many of his goods ware originally made in Manchester, whence they were shipped to Germany to be stamped with the German trademarks. To the touching reproach that in that case "you Germans must get the credit for many things made in Germany' which you never make at all," the traveller cheerfully replied, "That is quite true. The English manufacturers won't send out goods, so we do it, and make a profit." The anecdote is commended to the notice of Sir Howard Vincent.

7iet‘erss to the Otlttor.

WORKS COMPETITION.

Sir,—l beg to take up a little space in your valuable paper to make a few remarks in reference to the above, and more particular:y to the match which took place a week last night,vlx., Fletcher's V. Cheshire Lines. It has generally been the hoist of the Warrington specta- tors that; they always give fair play to the opposing club. They do not extend this advantage to Fletcher's team as they hooted our players in a manner that was painful to hear, and I am sure our play did not call for the same, but there seems to be some antipathy against our team because, I presume, we have so many old players. If this is the reason the Works Competition Committee can soon remedy it by debarring both past and present players, bat if this was done I venture to think the competition wi.uld then be of the tin can dribble order with a consequent fall in the gates. I would also like to state that these old players have stood by the club for years, and are always ready to fill a gap in the teams when the Works Competition Juniors are conspicuous by their absence ;as proof of this J. W. Evans has done so more than a dozen times this season. Now to come to the rules of the competition which state that this season's selected first or A teams are not eli- gible.We protested against Cheshire Lines on the ground that they had played a man who had been selected and played for A team, and had also been told by the sub committee that he was not allowed to play. I understand that air teams had equally good cause for protest, but to our surprise on Monday last the War- rington Committee overode their own rule and dismissed the objection saying the games most stand without giving any reason. I ask is this fair, not only to the clubs thrown out but also to those left in to thus completely alter the rules at this period of the competition as those left in can now play who they like. I should also suggest that only official referees he allowed to take charge of the games as some of the amateurs who officiate in this capacity seem to think that the clubs should play touch and held, thus not giving either teams a chance to show their scoring abilities ; and their decisions in other ways are, to say the least, considerably off. This letter is not written in a spirit of vexation but in the interest of the competi- tion in Warrington, as I think its popularity will soon wane unless better handled than it is at present. Another matter that should have the consideration of the committee is that the sub-commi:tee to work these competitions should be chosen from members not inter- ested in any works, as the majority of the present sub- committee are connected with works which have teams in the competition. Ido not for a minute suggest that they would be unfair but if they were not connected with any works they could have no inclination to lean to either side. As a proof of the love our men have for the game we will select a team to olav the winners of the medals the net proceeds of tho match to be handed to the District Nursing Fund or other deserving charity under any official referee, but we certainly will not take the field any more under any amateur referee. Thank- ing you in anticipation for inserting the above. Yours

===== 0002601_18980415_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN W. MILLS.

THE PROPOSED NEW POLICE BUILDINGS.

Sir,—ln a leaderette in " The Examiner" of the 28th of March you informed the public that "there are many signs that the realisation of the project is yet in the dim and distant future." The news would, no doubt, be very welcome to the ratepayers who feel the burdens of local taxation to be very heavy indeed. And knowing that the improvements in Bridge-et. will ere long make further demands upon them they are in no mood to sanction the outlay of a large sum for police purposes. But why should Warrington have a new police build- ing at all ! The present accommodation has served the town for a long time, and wt are not aware that Mr. Hunt, the late Chief Constable,ever made any complaint about its smallness or unsuitability. The population of the Borough has certainly risen a few thousand since the last census, but surely it does net follow that such increase should of necessity be accompanied by further developments of the crime and disorder with which the police have to deal. A large proportion of the addition is no doubt made up of children and young persons who are not contributors to the labours of the policeman. A police station whether large or small is neither an orna- ment nor a credit to a community, but, unfortunately, in the present conditions of our social life it is an insti- tution which cannot be altogether dispensed with. But something might be done to minimise it, and that with- out entailing any expense upon the ratepayers. It is well known both to our town counillers and magis- trates that most of the evils from wh .ch our constables are supposed to guard us have their origin in the public-bouses. Now as prevention is better than cure surely it would be a wise and humane policy to reduce the number of such houses and thereby promote , the best interests of the people, and at the same time bring police expenditure to the minimum. It might be objected that such a policy would militate against the pecuniary interests of the brewers and publicans. No doubt this would be so, but the brewers and publicans of Warrington are a very small fraction of the inhabitants indeed, and no righteous man would ever think of subordinating the peace,order and general welfare of 60,000 people to the ignoble profits of a hand- ful of liquor sellers. Well indeed it would be for the town if some good spirit would put it into the hearts of our justices to adopt the policy I have indicated. The process of reduction might be slow so as to give the Publicans time and opportunity to turn their attention to other callings. The cancelling of half a dozen licenses eery year would produce such good results that the scheme for a new police station would disappear alto- gether. Then what a change the policy would make in the condition of the working classes. As year by year the public houses became smaller by degreestand beauti- fully less the temptations to rpend their money for nought would become fewer and fewer ; their homes would be brighter aud happier, and peace and order wonld reign in the etrsets. And even the magistrates themselves would benefit by the change. Their time would no longer be spent in the miserable work of in- flicting punishment upon the unfortunate clients of the publicans, or in adjudicating upon thefts, murders and other crimes committed through drunkenness. And. better still, the blessing of multitudes rsady to perish would fall upon then.—Yours truly,

===== 0002601_18980415_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERAAN surroN AND COUNCILLOCZ

ROB BRTS. Sir,—Like many another man who has vainly tried to justify a defenceless position by introducing totally irrelevant matter, Mr. Sutton endeavours in his last letter to divert the attention of your readers from the subject in dispute by dragging in two questions which have not the remotest connection therewith. By what process of reasoning can the relations existing between the Liberal party and myself be said to have the slight- est association with Alderman Sutton's condi:let in re- gard to the Feathers Inn arbitration ? It is, perhaps, just as well to let Mr. Suttee knew that the feeling of the Liberals of Warrington towards me is as cordial to- day as it Ins been at any time during the a years I have been officially connected with thern,and within the past three weeks I have had twice offered to me the most important appointment which is in their power to confer. Then in regard to the contracts qaestion I This is by no means the first time that opponents of mine,when pulverised in regard to some other question, have had to resort to this old stalking-horse. I had to combat it seven years ago, and I suppose it will still occasionally be made to serve the purpose of those who, deficient in logical arguments, resort to throwing. Lot Let me say that many years ago I introduced a resolution which was adopted by the Council, and has since been confirmed and made a "standing order," requiring that all contracts for work or materials ex- ceeding the sum of £2O should be publicly advertised for, and the results printed in the Council Summons in the minutes of the committees concerned. My object was to enable every ratepayer who chose to tender to have the opportunity of doing so' and also to prevent collusion and favouritism. If I had been anxious to make use of my position as a member of the Corporation for the benefit of my firm, I should have adopted a very different plan. One effect of my resolution has been that no tender sent in by my firm has ever been ac- cepted by the Corporation, unless it has been in the in- terest of the ratepayers that it should be so accepted, and as a matter of fact in nearly every inflame the tenders of my firm have been accepted simply because their prices have been the lowest, and in no single case has any order been sent us at the slightest fraction higher in price than the material could be bought at from others. Parliament in its wisdom (and I presume I may without offence suggest that the average ef intelli- gence of the members, even of the present Houses of Parliament, is equal to that of my friend) has speci- ally exemrted shareholders in limited companies from the disqualifications which exist in regard to private individuals, so that Alderman Sutton has no lona stanii in raising this point, either on grounds of propriety or legality. And now to return to the main question. my difference with Mr. Sutton has arisen because he has done his best to defeat the object of the Corporation in their efforts to buy the Feathers Inn at a price which , the best expert evidence obtainable by us has declared ' to be fair and reasonable. I say without hesitation that Mr. Sutton's conduct is ineecusable,aud for this reason: The Corporation of Vi'arrington is a body et men elected by the ratepayers for the express purpose of adminis- tering the government of the town for the ad- community. It ia quite vantage of the whole true that as an alderman Mr. Sutton is not elected directly by the ratepayers, but he Is none the less bound by those Conditioos which are in honour obligatory upon every elected member of the Council. Mr. Sutton says that be has furnished no information to Mr. Wilson's counsel other than what was obtainable by anybody through the medium of the public press. If this statement is correct, what need was there for Mr. Sutton to supply our Opponents with a schedule of prices on a sheet of foolscap in hi 3 own handwriting • and fur- ther why should he have personally prompted Mr. Wil- son's counsel so repeatedly during the Cross-examina- tion of the witnesses for the Corporation ? It it not patent to the feeblest intellect that Mr. Sutton's weight as a witness on behalf of Mr. Wilson lies in the fact that

he is not only a member of the Council, ex-Mayor, Ise., but that he is still chairman of the Street Improvement Committee, and that up to November, 1295, he was a memb.r ef the Property Purchasing Committee ? Since this timo Mr. Sattoa has acted in a professional capacity for various owners concerned in the Bridge- street improvement, and I am perfectly amazed that he should veature to asßert his disinterestedness in the face of such facts. Mr. Sutton's suggestion that I desire to interfere as te the manner in which he should conduct his private business is nonsense ,but this I will say : That when a member of the Corporation finds that his pri- vate interests clash with his public duties, as an honour- able man he is bound either to resign his seat in the Council or to give up the private business which will not permit of his unbiassed action. Every public man has or ought to give up something if he wishes to act congcientiouily up to the duties imposed upon him and to deserve and retain the respect of his fellow townsmen. It is as true to-day as when the words were first proclaimed, that " No man can serve two masters." Let your readers try and imagine for a moment the result which would follow a general accep- tance by the members of the Council of the attitude which Mr. Sutton takes up! What would be said if any one of the paid officials of the Corporation were to be found assisting anybody in an action against the Cor- poration ? I imagine such an official would be instantly dismissed and rightly so,because he would be held to be disloyal to his employers—the ratepayers. What differ- ence is there in the terms :of appointment, except that of salary—between the officials of the Corpora- tion and the members of the Council? We are all servants of and trustees for the rate- payers, and our duties are similar and per- fEctly obvious. Let ma say, in conclusion, that I am not now and never have been, chairman of the Property Purchasing Committee (the hononr belongs to the Ex-Mayor, who has faithfully attended to his oner- ous duties), and that my colleagues, who are perfectly unanimous, have done everything which as reasonable and conscientious servants of the town they could pos- sibly do to avoid the expense and anno7ance of arbitra- tion proceedings ;and in a large measure they have been successful, as is shown by the fact that out of 12 differ. eat properties ache iuled for the purposes of the Bridge. street improvement scheme, no less than seven have been already acquired and these at figures which show a reduction upon the claims originally made by the owners of many thousands of pounds. Secrecy as to the action of the committee has for perfectly obvious rea- sons been absolutely necessary, but when the time arrives that the committee may without prejudice to the interests of the town, discicse the whole of their pro. ceedings, I am perfectly confident that our constituents will accord us their gsneral and emphatic approval.— Yours, &c.,

H. ROBERTS.

WESLEYAN FOREIGN MISSIONS.

The annual foreign missionary services in connec- tion with Bold-street Wesleyan Church were held on Sunday and Good Friday. Missionary sermons were preached on Sunday, morning and evening, by the Rev. William Potts, superintendent minister. Collections were taken at each service on behalf of the Wesleyan Foreign Missionary Society. On Good Friday morning, a missionary Service was held in the church, commencing at 11 a.m. The preacher for the occasion was the Rev. Jabez Bridge, of the Hull Mission, and a re'urned mis- sionary from West Africa. At night, a well- attended public meeting was held. The roceedings were presided over by Mr. John Hartley, and he was supported on the platform by the Revs. Jabez Bridge, William Potts, G. Harbottle, and W. G. Triggs. and Messrs. William Silcock, and John Taylor. After a hymn, and prayer by the Rev. W. G. Triggs, The Rev. W. Porrs in introducing Mr. Hartley, said they were exceedingly glad that Mr. Hartley had con- sented to take the chair. He did not think there was anyone in the circuit who had a better right to it, through his long amclation with that sanctuary and the aojoining school,. . _ _ _

The'Cliarn.meN called upon the Rev. G. Harbottle to present the report. In doing so Mr. Harbottle said the rmorts for 1897 were not yet to hand, and they most therefore take the 1898 report. The total income was £127,858 7s. sd, which was £129 13s. 51, in advance of the income of the previous twelve months. The debt of £963 83. 101, had been reduced to £834 153 31 The total amount subscribed by the V.T.srriagton circuit was £192 14s. lid. In addition to that the Women's Auxiliary raised £l4 14s 9i. (Applause.) In the mission field they had 230 central stations, and 370 missionaries and assistant missionaries. Other paid missionary ser- vants numbered 2,859, and there were also 5841 un- paid aeente. The accredited church members num. bered 44,573,and there were 9,895 on trial. The scholars attending day and Sunday schools numbered 88,542. Their missions in Germany had been transferred to the Methodist Episcopal Church of America. In Italy they were maintaining their missions in groat efficiency. He was sure none of ttPm would like the Wesleyan Missionary Society to withdraw from such a Roman Catholic country as Italy. After referring to the work in Spain and India, the rev. gentleman proceeded to refer to China. He remarked that the present difficulties in China had boon precipi- tated by missionary work. It was owing to the murder of a missionary there that the German Emperor seivd a port, and that hastened the action of Russia. He felt that these changes which were happening in China must have an immense influence on the progress of the work of Goi in that land. In conclusion he expressed the wish that the collection would be as good as on the missionary anniversary last year. The amount subscribed in that circuit during 1897 was £243 Is. TJ. which was £1 103. in advance of the previous year, but by retrenchment in expenditure they had been enabled to send £5 103, more to the Mission house. (Applause.) Speeches were afterwards delivered by the Chairman and the Rev. Jabez Bridge and others.

PLAYING WITH CARTRIDdES.

On Thursday afternoon a little boy named Wood- ward, living in Earle-street, Earlestown, found a small mining cartridge, and with natural curio- sity wanted to see what was inside. Lie picked up a stone and hammered at the cartridge, and as might be expected, it exploded with a loud report. A man passing saw that the lad was hurt and took him home, but fortunately beyond a laceration of one Anger he had suffered no further injury.

ACCIDENT TO A BOY Al EARLESTOAN. On Tuesday afternoon a boy named Joseph At- kinson,aon of Mr. J. C. Atkinson, jeweller,of Bridge- street, Barlestown, was badly injured. A. light lurry belonging to Mr. Ince, grocer, Market-street, was coining down Bridge-street, at a fair speed, when tie boy Atkinson ran behind and began swinging on it. Unfortunately one of his logs got fast in a wheel and was drawn in and wedged be- tween the nave and spring before the horse could be stopped. When the lurry was brought to a stand it was found that the boy could not be extricated until the wheel was taken off. Dr. Mouncey was sent for and it was found that the lad's leg was very badly torn but fortunately no bones were broken. The wound was dressed, and ths boy is going on as well as can be expected. HOPE OF EA.ELESTOWN LODGE, 1.0.0. T.

A pleasant, evening was spent at the Co-opera- tive Hall on Monday evening, when C. T. Bro. T. Cutler, presided. The Secretary read a letter in- timating the visit of the Sutton Lodge, next Mon- day night, and several other letters were read. The programme for the evening was" Pound night and social evening." Bro. B. Barker, (secretary) being responsible. Bro. J. Warburton rendered in good style a recitation followed by a song from Bro. Singleton. Afterwards Sister A. Owen rendered in a pleasing way " Muther kissed me in my dreams.' It was agreed that a prize distribution take place next season, to the competitors of the " Essay Cam- petition" for which Bro. J. Woods promised three books, and that the C. T. Bro. T. Cutler be the pre- sewer. After other business the Lodge closed in the usual way.—The newly opened lodge at Haydack held their meeting in the Colliery schools, on Tues- day evening a good gathering being present from Earlestown, and a pleasant time was spent.

EASTER HOLIDAYS AT EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. _ _

The weather in this district on Good Friday, was in every way all that could be desired, excepting perhaps the slight inconvenience experienced by the clouds of dust which were raised by the strong wind. From early morning the sun shone brilli- antly, and large numbers were tempted out into the surrounding couAtry. Cycli.ite were in evidence in every direction, but their enjoyment was very much interfered with by the wind and dust. Several set out with the intention of going long distances,but many founa it a difficult task to make headway, and had perforce to considerably curtail their spin. Services were held at the various churches morning and evening, which were fairly well attended. On Saturday many people left the town for Liverpool and Manchester, and other places. Monday opened very Rae, but at noon rain began to fall, and with slight intermission con- tinued to fall during the remainder of the day. Many who had set out early on for cycling,returned home thoroughly drenched.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EIRLESTOWN.

[Br AN OCCASIONAL Corirmsemmasr]. The scheme drawn up by the Church party for the regulation of the proposed new voluatary school has to all intents and purposes died a sudden and violent death, such demise being brought about solely by the energetic action of the local branch of the Northern Counties Education League, stimula- ted by the Rev, F. E. Miller, and Dr. Latham, who, by the way, has developed wonderful qualities as a fighter in such matters. Its death was foreshad- owed at the recent Nonconformist meeting, when letters were read from two directors of Messrs. Evans and Co., the second laraeet ratepayers in the district, and further by an intimation from the London and North-Western Railway Company that the proposed school must be on the broadest pos- sible basis.

There has of course, as the natural outcome of this opposition, been much heart-burning amongst the denominational supporters,and threats of throw- ing up the whole business have been quite numer- ous. Still these threats do not in any way disturb the equanimity of the unseemlier' party, and they go on their way rejoicing,conscious of the fact that they have been victorious in securing so much from two of the " seven largest ratepayers." It is said also that Lord Newton does not altogether agree with the strictly sectarian basis of the scheme, and so far has withheld his signature from the agree- ment transferring the land. On all hands the Edu- cation League appear to have scored, and I am in- clined to think the majority of the people are with them. At any rate it is plainly evident the school will not be conducted on the lines laid down in the proposed scheme.

Another item in regard to education matters transpired last week, much to the surprise of many people. It was announced that the managers of Newton St. Peter's and Wargrave Day schools had decided to abolish school feds, and that from and after the 18th April these schools will be free. Trey have now issued a circular to this effect, but at the foot of the circular creeps in a request that parents will subscribe to the school funds. This will in some cases be tantamount to paying school fees,but, if I mistake not, parents will refuse to become sub- scribers. Seeing that there is no compulsion upon them at all, there will still ba confusion, and thee is no doubt the only proper way out of the diffi- culty will be to establish a School Board.

ST. PETER'S, NEWTON. The annual vestry was held in the Town Hall, New- ton, on Monday evening. The Rev. H. Monk, Vicar, presided, and there were present, Rev. W. Rowlands, (curet.), Dr. Watkins, Dr. Harold Watkins, Messrs. J. Liptrott, I, Bateman, R. Stuart. J. H. Lane Sta. He could only say there should be no discussion on this question. Any complaint must be made to the wardens, and then to the rector, and if they would not be satisfied with his decisien they must ap- peal to the Bishop, and he would decide whether there was to be any alteration. LIVELY PROCEEDINGS AT BLYDOCIC. The annual meeting of the pariahioners of St. James'. Haydock, to transact the ordinary vestry business was called for Thursday evening. The meeting was held in the Schoolroom, and was presided over by the, Rev. F. J. Ireland, vicar, there being a very large attendance of parishioners—The Vicar, having read the notice con- vening the meeting, said the next business was to ap- point wardens and sidesmen.—A parishioner asked if io was not intended to read the minutes of the last Easter vestry or to submit the accountB.—Tbe Vicar said no minutes were to he read nor accounts submitted as they were not ready.—Mr. J. Hilton said it seemed very un- I business like to have no minutes read or accounts sub- ' mitted.--Tne Vicar: If you say another word I leave the chair and adjourn the nteeting.—Mr. Hilton per- sisted in his protest, and the Vicar declared the meeting r bead, and left the room. Immediately he had done so, the lights were turned out and the meeting was left in setni-darkness.—A parishioner moved that the people's warden take the chair. This was seconded and uproariously agreed to.— Mr. J. Robinson then occupied the vacant chair and said he felt himself in a somewhat queer position in be- ing asked to preside over that meeting. He very deeply regretted that the Vicar had felt it his duty to leave the chair so abruptly. He thought many of thorn could ro- man:it:el almost for ages.—The Vicar returning and ad- dressing the reporters said "This is not ts go in the papers,not a word. I am here as chairman of the school managers and the meeting is over."—Mr.Robinson: You have called the meeting here to-night and you have ab- ruptly left the Chair, and, as I think improperly. The meeting has elected me chairman.and I shall go on with the meeting. (Cheers.) I think it is very unfair of you Mr. Ireland. I wrote you a cordial letter,l held out the hand of friemlship,and after two years tryingova are to be tre s' ad in We Manner. I think it is alm-ist unbear- able.—The Vicar : We had a meeting of the managers in the next room,and we request you to leave the room. (Laughter.)—Mr. Robinson : I think I am in order and I decline to leave the meeting. (Cheers.)—A parishioner: Let him fetch the police. (Laughter.)—The Vicar ulti- mately left the building accompanied by his supporters. —Mr. Rabin:lon then proceeded to say that it was the bounden duty of the 'Vicar to respect the majority of the people of Flavdock, and he (Mr. Robinson) had a letter from the Bishop stating that two of the three points he (the speaker) had raised in his communication to the Bishop were perfectly valid, and that Mr. Ireland was acting illegally,and Mr. Ireland had been admonished by his Lordship, He (Mr. Robinson came there that night thinking they were to have a nice quiet meeting, and seeing that accounts were not ready, he thought Mr. Ireland would have seen the wisdom of adjourning the meeting, until the accounts had been audited and were ready. (Hear, hear.) Ile thought it was unbearable. He did not want to hurt Mr. Ireland in any degree. He had not seen the accounts for the whole 12 months and the money had been spent without his sanction. Seeing that the V car had been told by the Bishop that his acts were illegal, it seemed so him that there was nothing left but placing the matter in a solicitors hands,and hav- ing it fought out. (Hear, hear.) tie came there prep•red to say nothing at all thinking that the Vicar was still considering, and that the Vicar would at the last moment meat them. Even now, he could assure them that he felt it very difficult to proceed further, and he was afraid of over stepping his duty in saying anything further on this matter. TEN CHURCH COUNCIL.

Th 3 Chairman said t-h-e--Church Council had taken up the subject of a screen for the west end ef the church. The idea was to collect funds for the provision of a screen for the west end of the church as a tribute to the memory of Dr. Robinson. They felt that nothing had I been done to perpetuate the memory of one who was de- servedlv loved and respected throughout the whole place. —Mr. Foulkes hop3d the screen would never be put up. It was Ft step towards making the chancel of the church. —The Chairman (interposing) said he might explain to Mr. Foulkes that it was not intended to put the screen at the chancel and. The idea was to put a screen at the west end to keep out the draughts.—Mr. Foulkes asked whether a brass tablet would not be more in keeping with the wishes of the deceased gentleman.—Mr. Linaker pointed out that the screen would keep out the draught, and would serve a very useful purpose. Any- one who had stood at the west end of the church could testify to the fearful draughts which were experienced at times.

VOTIS OF THANKS.

Votes of thanks were accorded to the churchwardens and the sidesmen for their services during the year.—Mr Linaker also proposed a vote of thanks to the ladies fer their services in decorating the church on festive occa- sions. Notwithstanding the usual protest they would have, he moved that the best thanks of the vestry be tendered to the ladies.—Mr- Reynolds seconded, and Mr. Frith supported.—Mr. Faulkes proposed as an amend- ment, that the ladies keep the flowers at home. Ho con- sidered they were not in harmony with the teaching of the scripture. Fie Yore when introduced into a place of worship, were a part of the secret-satanic plot of tte Ritualists, or,as the "Tablet" described them, " the all- round traitors "of the Church of England to bring it , back again to the yoke of Popery. Tho secret society of St. Osmond, connected with the Church of England, had actually got a service for "driving the devil out of flowers or branches." Ritualistic young ladies especially would now need to be very careful The priest sprinkled the flowers and leaves with holy water. This was the testimony at Dr. Walsh on the "Secret History of the Ozferd movernent."—Mr. Linaker objected to these re- marks.—Mr. Foulkes continuing said he gave the Vicar notice on the 2ed April that he would brim" before the meeting certain matters which had appeared in the Parish Magazine.—The Chairman said the mat- ters of which Mr. Foulkes gave notice were matters that had nothing to de with the vestry, and, therefore,he be4-ged to close the meeting.— Mr. Foulkes: If you do not allow me to say anything I shall just hand over what I intended to say to the re- porters. (Laughter.)—No one seccnded the amend- ntent, and the motion was carried.—Mr. Foulkel : Are you prepared to allow me to speak on the note I wrote 7' The Vioar : No, I ant not prepared to allow you to speak en the note you sent me. As the businaas of the meeting is over I declare the meeting closed. —Mr. Foulkes : Excuse me, it is not quite over yet.—A vote of thanke was accorded to the Vicar for presiding, and the persons present witk the exception of Mr. Foulkes dispersed._

"Yet, some men are such fools that they think the worst thing which can happen to them is death." "Oh I it is terrible to hear you talk like that, Dennis! " cried his aunt. "You, who are so young, ought to think life a very good thing and I ought to be anxious to live a long while, and tk see your baby a grown-up woman—perhaps married woman with children of her own, and— " Come, Maria, don't anticipate events so much," broke in Mr. Hughes in a mildly expostulatory manner. "I only want to put dear Dennis in good spirits." "Prophesying that his baby girl will grow up to have children of her own, can scarcely have the desired effect." Dennis smiled a bitter, disappointed smile. After a momentary silence Mrs. Hughes observed nervously: "I think, from what Dr. Bolton said, that he wishes Grace to leave home to-morrow, if possible. Would you mind her doing so, Dennis ? " "Certainly not; now that matters have progressed so far, the sooner the doctor's suggestions are carried out the better." "That is just what I think," she replied, rising; "and I will go iind tell Grace that it is all settled, and that she has your full consent to come home with us to-morrow. She will be so glad to bear it." Dennis coloured. "Glad to hear it I " he mentally repeated. What cutting sarcasm those kindly-meant words contained! (Yo be continued.)
